Acknowledgments to the
First Edition
‘TT VHERE is more published material on the way in which the Treasury
_ + and other Government Departments take their decisions than is
"generally realized, if only one is Prepared to hunt around for it. I have
Placed at the end of the book some of the sources I have found most useful,
= All the same, any work of this kind must be heavily dependent on
Conversations with men and women at the centre of public affairs. In the
“course of preparing it, I have had lengthy interviews with about sixty or
Seventy different people, a few of whom I have bothered more than once,
“Since most of them must remain anonymous, it would be better not to
‘Single out any of them. But I would like to take this opportunity of
anking these extremely busy people for their very generous help, without
which the book could not have been written,
| Ina different category, I should like to thank the Information Division
"of the Treasury, which went out of its way to assist me in my efforts by
"arranging interviews and giving me all the information which it properly
ould, even though my requests often came at very short notice. Officials
with whom I have spoken have been helpful on factual matters within the
imits imposed by the bar against their discussing their advice to, or rela~
tions with, ministers. In absolving them and the Information Division
from any responsibility for what appears here, I should make it clear that
‘the help they gave me was confined to Part I of the book. Even here it
Teptesented only a small part of my material, the greater part of which was
derived from other sources.
_ Ishould like to thank the many friends and colleagues who have helped
Me at various stages. A special debt is due to Mr Nigel Lawson and my
brother Mr Leon Brittan who both read every word of my original draft
and discussed it with me in great detail. I am extremely grateful to them
tor the patience and speed with which they read my manuscripts and for
their useful and pertinent comments. Professor Alan Day made some very
helpful comments on Chapter 5, and on the first two Appendixes to
‘Chapter 3, and Mr David Henderson did the same with Appendix III of
hat chapter. My brother also gave me some most valuable editorial
assistance with the structure and organization of the book.

I should also like to express my gratitude to the Financial Times and the
Observer for the valuable insight I gained into the evolution of economic
policy, with the first as Economic Correspondent, and with the second in

so different from the commonly held idea of the makeing oO pelicy is
objects that it needs to be brought out very explicitly eens
extent to which there really is a choice between growth and oe es
stability requires more careful discussion than I originally gave tt The
whole subject can, I now believe, be expressed less tendentiously in t .
of the trade-off in all costs and benefits between alternative policies . ae
margin. Expressed’ in these terms the arguments for allowing dae
rates to vary—preferably in gradual amounts over the eke at eae
treating them as immutable objects of worship, are even stronger than I

due to holding on to outmoded parities for too long, rather than to the
changes that have belatedly been made.
‘Planning’ is discussed in Chapter to. This is clearly the area where
there has been the main shift of views between the two editions. The kind
of ‘planning’ championed in the first edition did not imply detailed state
intervention or control, but involved (a) the setting up of the National
Economic Development Council to act as a pressure group in favour of
growth when this conflicted with other priorities, and (b) a ‘virtuous
confidence trick’ designed to influence business expectations and invest-
ment. The demand management policies which ought to have accom-
panied this kind of planning should, I now believe, have been stated
differently to the way in which I stated them in 1964. It is nevertheless
still possible that aspect (D) might have had a marginally beneficial effect,
had confidence not been undermined by the priority given to maintaining
the $2-80 exchange rate for as long as possible. But it is mainly because it
was such a failure under aspect (a) that the whole planning movement
appears with hindsight a bad tactical mistake, which actually delayed the
policy reappraisal desired by its adherents. y
Contrary to some widely held impressions, [ was never in favour of
having two rival economic Ministries, and warned in the first edition of the
pre-Keynesian error of supposing that there were two separate subjects

PROLOGUE | xix
ing too warmly any North Atlanti ; :
Bere narrowed economic arguments. oP unde ao ae
early 1969 there were one or two signs that isti ic
Fe a Ee adore But, ifand when full economic ener ee
Will be a danger of slipping, back into old attitudes, which, have aes. all
the weight of tradition behind them. The view recalled here may sound
<nsular OF ‘little European’. But those who think so might ponder how low
Britain’s real influence in the world eventually sank as a result of the
orthodox, outward-looking overseas and financial policies of the postwar
period. While British leaders rushed from capital to capital on self-
appointed international peacemaking missions, the rest of the world was
more conscious of the rustle of their begging bowl, their utter dependence
on the USA, and their inability to impose their will on the breakaway
Rhodesian regime, still less to affect the course of events in the Middle or
Far East. The climax came in November 1967, with a devaluation which
in all but the technical sense was forced, and which it had been the main
object of policy of both parties to avoid. This was followed, within two
weeks, by the second French veto on the Common Market application—
this time on the plausible pretext of Britain’s economic unreadiness. None
put the incurably blinkered can seriously believe that all would have been
well had a Conservative Government not been in power up to 1964 or a
Labour Government not been in power in subsequent years.

The best that Governments can do to help people recover a healthy
patriotism and sense of national pride is to concentrate on the welfare of
the inhabitants of these isles. This may seem a prosaic form of patriotism,
but it is the main alternative both to outmoded ideas of Britain’s inter-
national position and to the narrow-minded xenophobia into which this
country seems at times inclined to sink. Britain will eventually find a new
and worthy role among nations, but the sooner the present preoccupation
with the subject ceases, the sooner this role will be found.

a ae
The Changing Whitehall
Background
nd the Career Structure 0 Training and Recrui -
Specialists and All-rounders O The Civil Service Depa ae a rs
Whitehall at Work O ‘Treasury Characteristics O Ministers he
and Officials 0 Regularizing the ‘Irregulars’ 0 The Secrecy :
Obsession O The Crucial Issues ; he
HE best introduction to the work of the Treasury is to glance at the on te
T cwit Service of which it is part. Ee
The Fulton Committee, which reported in 1968, began its description maT
with the words ‘Civil Servants work in support of Ministers in their
public and parliamentary duties.’* This statement is deliberately open-
ended. The work involves preparing plans and advising on policy, drafting “
regulations or Parliamentary Answers and producing briefs for Ministerial eT
speeches and conferences. There are motorways to be planned, weapons *
systems to be designed, and contracts to be placed. There are the less acer
glamorous jobs such as collecting tax and running employment exchanges,
which provide the public with its main impression of the Civil Service at
work.
These examples could be multiplied over many pages. There is, how-
ever, an important distinction to be made at the outset. The Fulton
Committee remarked : ‘Operating policies embedded in existing legislation
and implementing policy decisions take up most of the time of most Civil
| Servants.’ While perfectly correct for most Government departments, this
is much less true of the Treasury, most of whose work is concerned with
policy of one kind or another. The boundary line between the two kinds of
activity is, of course, a vague one. A Treasury official who recommends a |
change in credit restrictions to prevent consumer spending from diverging
from the Chancellor’s intentions is indeed suggesting new measures; but,
from another point of view, he is trying to enforce an existing policy. The |
expression ‘economic management’ indicates how blurred the distinction is. |
Nevertheless, compared with other Ministries, the Treasury is clearly }
at the policy-making end of the spectrum. This characteristic became even 4
more pronounced with the 1968 decision to hive off the responsibility for
running the Civil Service on to a new Department. For this reason, the
emphasis in the following pages is more on the central policy-making
* The Civil Service: Paras. 27 and 28, Cmnd 3638, 1968.

E sia found irrespective of label. right individual
shoul’ «articular classes promotion was normally base ae
EY eae st be fit to ei out al/ the jobs ‘which Bat eae
his class at the higher level. Such a system of ‘Promotion handicaps those
ho might have something important to contribute in a narrower range of
activities, while it benefits the man who is competent over the whole range
put not necessarily very inspired in any part of it. An earlier enquiry, the
Priestley Royal Commission of 1955, summarized rather well the qualities
required in an Adminstatyg class Civil Servant as the capacity ‘in an
unusual degree’ to ‘master and marshal detail in many different fields at
different times, to interpret effectively the ideas and policies of others,
and to operate a complex administrative machine. It is rare to find race
qualities in balanced proportion in one individual.’ It is probably a mistake
to insist on seeking the combination.

The Fulton Committee recommended a unified system of about a dozen
grades, with a horizontal division between groups but easier movement
between them, and with the higher posts open to all, irrespective of the
route by which they had come. The abolition of all classes was accepted
by the Prime Minister on 20 June 1968; but it would be wrong to suppose
that the mere replacement of one hierarchical system by another, however
improved, will automatically remove the defects endemic to large organi-
zations, in which it is always difficult to tailor jobs to individuals. Although
the ‘job evaluation’ so warmly recommended by Fulton is a step in the
right direction, it would not solve the problem in many of the higher posts,
where both the qualities required and the merits of particular individuals
are likely to be matters of keen controversy.

Fulton indeed missed one very obvious reform, which would probably
make more practical difference than the abolition of classes. This would be
to unify the Home and Overseas Civil Services. The case for unification is
particularly strong now that the commercial and economic aspects of
diplomatic work are so heavily emphasized. Under present arrangements,
these tasks are bound to seem chores which few diplomats can carry out
with first-hand knowledge. Equally, officials in Home departments too
often have to speculate, with no real background understanding, about the
overseas implications of British policies.

Such a unification would widen the horizon of both branches and would
have a genuine effect on social class barriers in the one area of Whitehall
where they still exist. The very ferocity with which the proposal is so often
resisted shows how near the bone it reaches. The fact that Fulton's terms

givers) raduates applying for the Administrative Ciy; :

Univer ost of the postwar period been ee eee sith
throws 1, which is primarily a written examination their own en
Methor ;) which involves general papers, personality tests ai ject, and
Metho {1 has tended to fall into desuetude, partly because tnd eae
Meta ea final year understandably shy away from two Ful see doce
er examinations. There were in the middle and late 1960s oe : a
pee each year in the Administrative Class and about nine qual
eta candidates. Method 1 provided little more than one-fifth of the
successful applicants in 19673, and the method may well in future be
restricted to a written examination in a few subjects, such as economics and
social studies, regarded as of particular relevance. This would also be a
gesture towards the majority of the Fulton Committee, who recommended
a preference for students of relevant’ subjects, while in practice keeping
the main method of recruitment open to all comers. A Civil Service Com-
mission follow-up enquiry showed that the marks scored in Method 2
were a much better indication of subsequent performance than marks
scored in Method 1.*

Until about 1965 the Civil Service Commissioners used to complain
that they were not getting enough candidates of the right quality; but the
position later became a little easier and by 1967 they were in fact able
to recruit as many Assistant Principals as they needed. It is possible that,
following Fulton, Method 2 will be extended and adopted to recruit a
larger number of graduates over a wider range of ability, including some
who will eventually be posted to lower grades than the normal successful
Administrative class entrant would have gone to in the past.

Table 1 shows the preference of successful entrants in a sample of recent
competitions. Although the Treasury was high upon the list of preferences,
it no longer had a clear lead over all other departments, and had strong
competition from the Department of Education and the Home Office in
particular. Candidates who came near the top of the competition had a
reasonable chance of going to the department of their choice.

In assigning new entrants, Whitehall works on the assumption that the
Treasury requires a high standard of intellectual sophistication. In prewar
days no one was appointed to the Treasury as his first post, but some came
earlier to it than others, Today suitability is judged by performance in the
entry competition. Senior Treasury officials also have a better chance than
most of their colleagues of being sent out to run another department as
Permanent or Deputy Secretary. Of 24 posts of Permanent Secretary rank
outside the Treasury or Cabinet Office in 1968, 10 were held by men with
ten or more years’ Treasury service (a qualification also enjoyed by the
Cabinet Secretary himself),

The problem of providing approved courses for r eally senior officials i
inherently more difficult. But a beginning was made in 1967 with a pa

The suggested Civil Service College would amount to an extension of }
the activities of the existing Centre for Administrative Studies, rather than 1
an entirely new concept. A college would have a permanent staff instead of
relying on lecturers recruited for a temporary period. It would take over
some of the training hitherto provided by individual departments, and it
might also be equipped to provide the residential seminars for more senior
officials already mentioned. In a Commons debate on 21 November 1968 }
the Prime Minister announced that the former Civil Defence College at \
Sunningdale would be used as the first residential centre. A non-residential }
teaching institution would continue in London, and in due course a third
centre might be established in one of the regions. The Committee’s more
ambitious ideas include giving the College research functions and opening
it to students outside the Civil Service,
The most important difference between the British and French systems
of training Civil Servants is no longer in the amount of formal instructions, 1
but on the practical side. During his first year at the Ecole Nationale, an }
aspirant to the French Civil Service is sent to assist one of the Prefects \
who represent the Central Government in a provincial centre. The psycho- 1
logical motive for sending him is not disguised. ‘It is to give the student a 1
complete break from all past associations and ideas, to force the metro- |
politan man to live in the provinces, and to bring him into contact with |
those parts of French society which, once started on his career, he will \
have no further chance to contact or explore.’* Yet in this area, Fulton is |
surprisingly vague; simply saying that ‘as many as possible’ of the young |
entrants should spend a spell at places where they can meet individual )
members of the public, and should gain experience of work outside the |
Service, A different point rightly emphasized by Fulton is that the lack of 1
familiarity with foreign languages is an increasing handicap in the Home |
Civil Service, but again there are no specific recommendations. \
There was indeed a move in the mid-1 g6os to promote short-term inter- \

jor Chapman, op. cit., pp. 11-16. An account of the French, Swedish and \
ican Civil Services is contained in an Appendix to Fulton. |

here- |
spECIALISTS AND ALL-ROUNDERS
‘ulton’s Committee’s denunciation of the philosoph: ‘
to generalist or ‘all-rounder’) aroused a nit deal of ee
resentment among the Civil Service. First of alll it passed over too lighth
the change of sentiment and practice which had already begun by te
beginning of the 1960s. Secondly, it swallowed, without any pinch of salt
whatever, the fashionable slogans about professionals versus amateurs, Of
course more ideas and more expertise would have been beneficial in
Whitehall in the 1950s and even the 1960s, but they are not necessarily the
same thing as formal professional qualifications. The organized profes-
sions, such as law and medicine, with their rigid demarcations, examina-
tions and entrenched conservatism, have not always, to put it mildly, been
forces for enlightenment. Some of the worst Whitehall appointments have
gone to officials with admirable paper qualifications and relevant experi-
ence, who would have scored very highly in any Fulton-type appraisal.
Having said all this, it must be admitted that Fulton found some severe
shortcomings in the level of expertise. This applied in particular to the
frequency with which officials were removed from one job to another,
often among entirely unrelated activities. The sample of Administrators
(other than Assistant Principals) interviewed had spent on average 3:2
years in their last completed jobs. For Principals (who have to write many
of the working papers) it was as little as 2-5. The result was that no sooner
had someone really learnt his job than he was getting ready to move to a
new task, The average time-span of dealing with a problem was larger
than the time spent by the Administrator in his post, and the knowledge of
imminent transfer undoubtedly discouraged too large an involvement in
background research (for example in investigating how other countries
were tackling similar problems).
A specific example given in The Managerial Consultancy Group's
Report (which appears as Volume 2 of Fulton) was that of a Principal
reviewing the investment programme of a nationalized industry. In the
first annual cycle he was himself learning the elementary facts. The
following year he could deal more confidently with the subject, but by then
he would be under consideration for a move—‘just at the time when he
would have acquired sufficient familarity with the routine to permit him
eee more deeply into such factors as the structure of ne
: ards of operating efficiency, its management and to apply analyti
chniques.”* There is no real inconsistency between advocating @

that a fairly high proportion of its members would continue to be academ-
ics on temporary appointment. The abolition of all ‘classes’ in favour of
‘occupational groupings’, as recommended by Fulton, should make it
easier for economists who stay on in Whitehall toreach Permanent Secretary
level if their talents and interests develop in that direction.
‘At the end of the 1960s there were still some unresolved problems about
the organization of Whitehall economists. One of them was their relation
to statisticians, hitherto an entirely separate ‘class’, but often employed in
related and overlapping work. A great deal of the analysis of the current
situation and of short-term prospects, which was performed in the }
Treasury by economists, was carried out by statisticians in the Board of |
Trade. There was a case for unifying the occupational groups—not to \
force all economists to be professional statisticians or vice versa, but to |
recognize the existence of a whole spectrum of different aptitudes, abilities \
and interests in this broad area, which cannot be chopped into two con- |
venient halves. \
For the normal Civil Service Administrator, the Fulton idea is that he |
should have an area of special knowledge where he can haye a fruitful
dialogue with the professionals and make use of their contributions,
without pretending to be one himself. This is surely a reasonable goal; in
the economic sphere it has for some time been formulated in terms of Civil
Servants being ‘economically literate’. Fulton is also wise to suggest that
the areas in which Civil Servants should specialize should be based on
subject matter rather than department. But the tentative recommendation
that two broad areas of specialization should be marked out, one in the
€conomic and financial, the other in the social spheres, is most unfortunate,
For it is based, if one may caricature just a little, on the view of economics
as a study concerned with producing material largesse, and sociology as @
ee 5a causes into which some of this largesse should be

policy than with detailed execution. Unfortunately Fulton felt unable to
say more ee tt ice. body should make an ‘early and thorough
, f the whole .
Te suggestion Fulton did make was that most departments
should have a Senior Policy adviser of at least Deputy Secretary rank with
direct access to the Minister. He would usually be a career Civil Seryant
with special knowledge of a particular field, although occasionally he might
be brought in from outside. His main job would be to look beyond day-to-
day issues, ‘prepare for the future’, and make sure that the researches of
his staff were not overlooked in current policy.
This proposal certainly points in the right direction. It was argued
earlier in this chapter that it was a mistake to seek in one man the complete
combination of qualities traditionally required in a good Civil Servant. At
Permanent Secretary level, moreover, the sheer range of duties is too much
for one man, however diverse his talents. Fulton lists four functions: advice
to the Minister; acting as managing director of the operations of his
department; responsibility for its staff and organization; and personal
responsibility as Accounting Officer for all departmental expenditure.
Some regrouping is highly desirable, although it is likely to be strongly
eras in Whitehall where the mystique of the ‘Permanent Secretary’ is
igh.

One can, however, have reservations about the implied identification of
the Policy Adviser with crystal-gazing and long-term research, which
could easily be a recipe for impotence. Inthisrespectitisall too reminiscent :
of the kind of thinking that led to the division of the long and the short
ae after 1964, between the DEA and the Treasury. The trouble with
he ae for Tesearch and statistical investigation is that it can too easily
palit P ae by Politicians or officials who want to avoid making difficult.

ae choices, It can in this form become just a modern version of the Nee
Snoured expedient of setting up a committee. As Fulton concedes,

oe gh it is, has multiplied threefold since the war) sushi
Sune now houses the Cabinet Secretary, who can w, a aeee a
er , to No. 10 Downing Street without once Venturing out sae the
ora ieee itself now occupies a singularly charmless 1908 building
Pome the ‘New Public Offices 3 one irreverent inmate now retired
called it ‘the mausoleum » but its most common title is simply the ‘Great
George Street Front’. The exterior of the Great George Street Front has
been described as a gentlemanly copy of the Italian style, which looks as ]
though it had been designed by Inigo Jones when suffering from indiges- :
tion. The interior resembles a municipal hospital. One end of the building, ;
containing the Ministry of Housing, faces Whitehall. The other end, |
overlooking St James’s Park, has been used by a variety of departments /
and in 1964 was given over to the Department of Economic Affairs, The /
Treasury is between the two in the long stretch looking out on to Great
George Street and Parliament Square.

The most highly regarded younger Treasury officials tend to circulate
between the Treasury itself (where they will have done spells of duty in
the Private Offices of Ministers and senior officials) and the very small
staffs who make up the Cabinet Office and the Prime Minister’s own band
of personal secretaries. Mr Harold Wilson made efforts to reduce the
Treasury role in the staffing of both of these other offices; and it is not yet
clear whether the new Civil Service Department will continue to be part
of this inner circle. Nevertheless there is every sign that this nucleus,
which in a very real sense has been the centre of the Government machine,
will continue in being. The close personal contacts involved do have
Some use in short-circuiting a great deal of cumbersome and archaic
protocol.

Despite these contacts, and a few personal friendships, Treasury officials
do not see a great deal of each other socially in their own homes. A few
Senior officials, especially on the Overseas side, have to attend a large
number of official lunches and cocktail parties, mostly to meet foreign
ville and Embassy representatives, But these officials are very much in

minority,
__ Ahandful of the more senior Treasury men do come together for more
ae and Private dinner discussions at one or two dining groups,
joi a a Political Economy Club and the Tuesday Club, where they are
vm Seton Rie ae and business figures and one or two

i tal journalists.

y and the National Institute will not always have identical views :

Ota argument for the ‘irregular’ is that, as he does not owe his appoint-
ment to the Civil Service heads and his Place in the hierarchy is less
closely defined, he ought to be ina better position to question accepted
departmental attitudes at the vital stage before policy alternatives are
presented to Ministers. Outside critics, especially where the subject is
technical rather than crudely political, have little Opportunity of influenc-
ing policy in a specific way. Not having seen the confidential minutes of
all meetings and not knowing the stage which departmental thinking has
reached, they have little chance of making headway against a Minister’s
official advisers. They can only hope to make an impact if they are taken
into a department as ‘antibodies? and participate in the real discussions
where policy is made, (Many of the established Civil Servants who were
onal Most opposed to the new recruits came to regard them as a
stimulating influence.)

bear a castrated and intellectually impoverished look, ee

HE first job of all Finance Ministries all over the world is oa
Ae. to finance Government spending. Although the British Trem
ury is far too sophisticated to proclaim so simpliste a doctrine, itis, lik ke al
; other Finance Ministries, still very much concerned to restrain Over,
Ment spending and combat tax Proposals that threaten the reveny
Anyone trying to predict the Treasury’s behaviour will do well to bear thi.
in mind before plunging too deeply into the more sophisticated do trines
which the Department Proclaims and which also influence its aCtivities
One preliminary difficulty in outlining the work of the Treasury is tha
because of its Breat age, it is one of the few Whitehall departments whose
functions are not defined by statute. As a result, its Powers and reg pon-
sibilities have always been the subject of confusion and argument, e
SOME PERMANENT SECRETARIES “
Fora large Part of the twentieth century the Treasury’s Tesponsibility for
Tunning the Ciyil Service was a main Preoccupation of its Perman ent
Secretaries and affected its whole ethos. This was far and away the prin ci
Pal interest of Sir Warren Fisher, an unusual figure who was Permanent
Secretary to the Treasury from 1919 to 1939. His main object in life was a
unified and, in Practice, self-regulating Civil Service; and he would on
recasion send out circulars about the four Crown Services, the Amny,

TREASURY ORGANIZATION
In view of the Treasurys wide ramifications it is hardly surprising that it
should possess a number of lesser ministers of a higher rank than the
normal junior minister. The oldest of these posts, that of Financial
Secretary, has existed since the eighteenth century. The holder has certain
well-established duties; for example the Financial Statement, which
*ppears at the time of the Budget, is issued in his name. But his tradi-
nae Tesponsibilities for supervising public expenditure were taken over
oe Chief Secretary, a superior post created in 1961. The Chief
“tary actually sat in the Cabinet until the 1964 election, and was again
Ws there by Mr Wilson in 1968. There has sometimes been a fourth
gauistet at the Treasury, who until 1964 carried the title of Economic
Gow This was allowed to lapse in the first few years of the Labour
State nen but was also revived in 1968 under the title of Minister of
ec.
mo allocation of duties between the various subsidiary ministers at the
“asury has been as fluid as their titles. Effective delegation has been
. :

3 THE ORGANIZATION OF ECONOMIC Poxicy fag

pas ROLE OF THE BANK
<peland has for centuries had a greater say j ?

The Bae ae Her Majesty’s Ministers. Tad at ee
rant 29°" ime the Chancellor's instrument in the City, and the Lsreein:
the sam City in government circles. Unfortunately there can often Hi
for ihe between the two roles. This may have nothing to do with ae
conflic t colour of the government of the day, but may for example reflect
polities on between the Bank’s interest in safeguarding the balance of
the ten _-which may, as in the mid-1960s, require control over overseas
payne cnt—and its role as a defender of the City, which naturally
Le d the freest possible movement of capital. There is a strong case
ae having 4 separate body which can argue uninhibitedly the case for the
City, leaving the Bank to concentrate on its monetary role. (A ‘City Liaison
Committee’ was in fact set up in the mid-6os to brief Mr Kenneth Keith,
the City representative on NEDG, and this could possibly form the
nucleus of such a representative group.)

Although the Bank of England is concerned to show that it is not a
Government Department (which is somewhat anomalous, for its role is
almost entirely governmental), there has been a big change in recent years
in its relations with Whitehall. In the days of Montagu Norman (who
reigned from 1920 to 1944) the Governor of the Bank would normally go
alone to see the Chancellor and no Treasury officials could be present.
Since the publication of the highly critical Radcliffe Report on the
Monetary System in 1959, the Bank has gradually been drawn more
tightly into the Whitehall committee network. For many purposes the
Bank is coming to behave more like a Government Department, but like
all Government Departments has some information that it keeps to itself.
An important remaining difference is that, unlike a Permanent Secretary,
the Governor of the Bank of England has the advantage of being able to
express his views in public as well as private, but unlike a Minister he
enjoys security of tenure for at least his five-year-period of office.

The gulf in ethos between the two institutions narrowed considerably in
the 1960s. The anti-intellectualism of the Montagu Norman and early
postwar periods diminished considerably. By 1968 two out of the three
executive directors concerned with policy were of an academic rather than
central banking background, and the Chief Cashier was a former Oxford
economics don who had come up via the Economic Intelligence Depart
ment. The Bank also began to try in the 1960s to attract graduates, with
onc Prospects more nearly comparable to the Civil Service than
One im a past. It is, however, wrong to overdramatize the changes.

Bank one Comite: there is still likely to be a contrast between. the
public eee Tepresentative, who may well have come straight from
repre ool and had a thoroughly practical training, and the Treasury
ative with a background of Oxbridge and the Ministerial Private

these tW° concerns of the Bank are at the bottom of Most of its Policy
Sore desens e interested in whether foreigners ; :
ae Bank val ie less easily reassured by theoretic nine * selling

weaken e the Bank of England 1s more concerned than the economic side
At ae with the size of the Government borrowing requirement For
of the ve the borrowing requirement the less the Bank has to borrow for
the Se ent in the gilt-edged market; and in addition, a low borrowing
the erementis believed to be good for confidence in sterling. Although the
re will advocate higher taxes to keep Exchequer borrowing down, this
Ban n only as the lesser evil. Successive Governors have felt Passionately
ee the evils of government spending and have devoted more of their
al “ lic exhortations to this than to any other single subject. Unfortunately
ie have been generous with broad doctrine and sparing of detailed
prescription. : v ant
Like all unchallenged monopolies, the Bank’s position as the sole source
of advice and expertise in the foreign exchange market is unhealthy.
Despite its undeniable technical competence, the Bank’s actions have by
no means always exhibited the unquestionable wisdom taken for granted
by its City and political admirers. There was, for example, the contrast
between the enormous support it gave to the forward market in the three
years up to November 1967, when there was always a good chance that the
no-devaluation gamble would come unstuck, and the subsequent complete:
departure from the forward market, when the parity was less unrealistic
than it had been for a long time. In addition it was not at all obvious
why, after devaluation, the Bank started dealings at the top end of the
$2:38-$2-42 range, at which holders of sterling would be aware that the
rate could only go downwards. Nor has the Bank’s role in the loss of
reserves on the Friday before the 1967 devaluation, when the foreign
exchange market remained open after Mr Callaghan’s non-committal
answer in the House of Commons, been entirely clarified; and it is not
easy to overlook the suggestion of a failure of morale in one or two parts
of the institution in the months immediately following devaluation.
s oa all these points, the Bank would certainly have strong counter-
de ae to put forward; and it would be quite wrong to attempt a
a Hh Verdict here. These controversies are mentioned simply to show
€se are issues on which more than one opinion is possible.

ae this is a good place to point out that the expression ‘gnomes’ has
no definite meaning, even as a colloquialism. It is sometimes used to refer
vaguely to the above series of organizations and meetings without attempt-
ing to distinguish between them, and sometimes to private individuals (by
no means all bankers or foreigners) whose feeling about currencies
influence movements of funds between countries. The identification of
‘gnomes? with Switzerland has its origin in the 1950s when the market in
‘transferable sterling? (a particular variety of inconvertible sterling long
since defunct) had its main centre in Ziirich. Swiss banks still play a role
n looking after the accounts of many international clients, but they are
only one of many elements in the international flow of short-term funds.
Nothing was more misguided, for example, than the procession of tele-
vision reporters in the 1960s to interview Swiss private bankers on their
views about the British economy—views which were in fact mostly
derived from the British Press. The creditors who impose conditions are

ECONOMISTS AND FORECASTERS a
Officials throughout the Treasury are, of course, handling eco nomics ;
the time, whether they know it or not (like Moliére’s M. Jourdain,
discovered that he had been speaking prose all his life). The Profession;
economists have, however, certain distinguishing characteristics. Th )
all members of the Economic Section and come under its Head, who hi
had varying titles at different times, including Economie Adviser to
Government and-Head of the Government Economic Service. Me
bers of the section customarily assemble in his room for a weekly mee
and tend to develop a corporate loyalty, ee
A handful of economists work with the public sector of finance sides of
the Treasury; but the majority work in their own specialized section. Th
may be called upon to Provide analysis or advice on any subject ranging
from the effects of a suggested tax reform to the behaviour of wag
There are also what Sir Alec Cairncross has called ‘odd jobs like writ
speeches, attending international conferences, coping with other econon
advisers, and trying to Tecruit more economic advisers.’* Without etern
vigilance these not entirely uncongenial tasks will take up most of the
available time, a
cq Lt job which, Tightly or wrongly, has become in Cairncross’s ords
the most important role’ of professional €conomists is that of forecastin
It is the one area where professional €conomists rather than career Ciy
Servants have, ever Since Cripps, taken the lead in advising ministers.
Current data on Production, employment, exports, and other variables ar
assessed as they come in, and are collected together in the Treasury
monthly economic ‘port, which has been published since 1967. Special
eae may, of course, be Written for much more limited circulation, if

economic ia m forecasts try to give a picture of how the nationalin ot
All sore ahead quarter by quarter; the February one will try ee
al we the behaviour of the economy for a two-year period from the en 1
ne previous year to the end of the following year. Each National
of t e Forecast (NIF) is accompanied by a World Trade and by a UK
3 ae _of-Payments Forecast. The Finance divisions playaspecial rolein
the whole balance-of-payments assessments. On world trade and British
exports, the Treasury has to lean heavily on background Papers prepared
by the Board of Trade, although it also takes account of the Bank of
England’s views of prospects in different parts of the world.

The June forecasts are intended as a check to see if the Budget calcula-

tions are working out as intended. They may give the Treasury its first
warning that something extra may have to be done. They have often, as in
1961, 1965, and 1966, led to deflationary packages more important than the
Budget itself. The October forecasts give Budget planners a useful first
guess on which to work, and the Public Sector officials some idea of just
hae severe to be in bargaining with the departments on the coming year’s
Estimates. They are also used to help decide whether the Chancellor will
need to do anything in the autumn without waiting for the Budget. There
is, in fact, no longer anything particularly unusual about measures which
amount to a Budget in all but name at any time of the year.

The medium-term projections normally go up to about five years ahead
and are completed in early summer, although fresh Projections may be
made at any time in response to special requests. Responsibility for these
assessments was transferred from the Treasury to the Department of
Economic Affairs in 1964; but there is a good chance that they will be back

| the Treasury either by the time this book appears, or soon afterwards.
An alternative possibility, long considered in Whitehall, is that a unified
8roup responsible for all forecasts should be transferred to the Cabinet
Office, where it could serve all Government Departments. (The Central
Statistical Office is already situated there.) The possibility of claiming for
Pe Telations Purposes that such a unit would be ‘directly responsible
Mon © Prime Minister” makes the Proposal a sensitive one politically.

es send in their preliminary revenue forecasts in time for these summer

os ie 6g been a very senior Executive Officer—is therefore able to work
rat bis first ‘Exchequer Prospects Table’, outlining revenue and expendi-
rure in the coming year.

Jn autumn the pace quickens, By early October, figures for public
snvestment—power stations, hospitals, schools, ete—have been agreed,
and in early November the autumn economic forecasts are ready. Their
aim is to predict the growth of demand and output in relation to the
country’s productive capacity, and the balance of payments, on the
hypothetical assumption that nothing is done in the Budget. This provides
the basis of a preliminary assessment of the sort of Budget in fact likely to
be required,

In the course of the autumn, papers are also presented on long-term
camomic objectives. Ideas for changing the tax system, perhaps abolishing
some taxes and introducing others, may be brought up in this context by
‘Treasury economists or other officials. These will often be opposed by the
Revenue Departments, who may have quite genuine administrative objec-
tions,

The representatives of the Revenue Departments take little detailed
part in the general economic discussions, confining themselves to a remark
here and there on the way they think business is moving, They do, how-
‘ver, put up papers in the course of the autumn or early winter on ‘the
tate’ of the various taxes. They might point out, for example, that the
revenue from beer and tobacco duties, on which Chancellors have relied
40 heavily in the past, is not likely to grow very quickly in the future
Owing to changing social habits. They will have their own ideas for replace-
one high may be different from those of the economists, but are at least
B ely to prevail, :

Ye he Way be ding to the theory which now governs Bud (he balance

atte _
confidencr forecasting, formal or by hunch, is nowadays at the centre f
Eon Budget exercise, whether Chancellors choose to admit itor bee
the whe oblems arise here when the balance-of-payments forecast points
The 1 een and the home economic forecast in another. There are no
in one rules for resolving such a conflict. Indeed, with the absence of any
eenerlly accepted balance-of-payments ‘regulator’, the conflict may be
nat to the Budget judgment is that the Treasury normally does not
think in terms of the rate of growth of output itself, but of the ‘pressure of
demand’ on the economy, as measured by the unemployment Percentage,
In the late 1960s it regarded a pressure of demand corresponding to
around 2:0 per cent as optimum, anything less it regarded as inflationary,
and anything very much more as an unnecessary waste of resources. If the
balance of payments is satisfactory, the Treasury will not over-exert itself "
against politicians who are prepared to allow unemployment to fall a little
below the target, although its warning will be on record. Conversely, if the :
balance of payments has been, or is likely to be, in deficit, the Treasury
will give it top priority and not worry overmuch if unemployment looks }
like slightly exceeding the 2:0 per cent level.
According to the doctrines which have reigned in the Economic Section, at:
if balance-of-payments equilibrium requires an unemployment rate very 4
much higher than 2:0 per cent, the price is too great to pay and devaluation 5
will be recommended. But every expedient will be adopted to avoid this \
unwelcome recommendation as long as possible. Increases in unemploy- 4
ment can be treated as temporary fluctuations; and attempts can be made :
to buy time by overseas borrowing or rephasing external debt repayments.
By the autumn of 1967, all these expedients had already been used up to +
and beyond any reasonable limits, and there was no alternative (short of a ] 4
policy of permanent trade restrictions) to a devaluation recommendation \
(which would, of course, have been given in an even more restricted .
gathering outside the framework of the Budget committee). : B
Two other points need to be made about the pre-Budget economic +
aE The first is that different measures affect the economy with \
; ey time-lags. Thcome-tax changes, which do not even begin to affect i
ie ‘xpayer until the following July, start slowly and then build up toa yo
ger effect. Taxes on goods take effect immediately, while hire-purchase \

ce meaning of such assessments is far from simple, They relate
basically to demand rather than to Exchequer revenue. Tax increases
which are paid largely out of capital or savings, such as increases in Cor-
poration Tax or levies on investment income, ought to be heavily discounted
in working out the economic impact of the Budget. Public understanding
is not helped when the Budget speech is entirely in terms of the revenue
raised and the effect on the Exchequer borrowing requirement, as was the
case in 1968, largely to impress overseas opinion. The amount of extra
revenue raised in that Budget was more than £goom. but the actual
amount the Chancellor had ‘taken out of the economy’ was later given as
just over £500m.
The meaning of even this last figure is ambiguous. Two calculations are
in fact made. To help the Chancellor select his package a table is prepared
of the effects of a large number of possible measures. This sets out for each
measure (2) the impact on revenue in the first year, (}) the impact in a full
| year, (c) the direct effect on demand at factor cost by the first quarter of the
next calendar year and (d) the effect on the retail price index. The demand E
calculation at this stage excludes indirect ‘multiplier’ and ‘accelerator’ |
effects but includes the effect on imports. Secondly the economic fore- /
casters adjust their estimate for ourput up to the end of the forecast period in E
the light of the package chosen. It was not entirely a coincidence that the |
he measures gave very similar results for both the 1968 and 1969 Budgets, |
ut this happy similarity cannot always be relied upon to obtain. |
% ay attempt to present the judgment in the more readily understood 4
impace te is full of ambiguities, such as whether it is in terms of 7 1
their fall - year or “in a full year’ (when the tax changes have ae
previ Tuit), or which changes are to be regarded as new and wl ;
‘ously foreshadowed. There is, in fact, a case for avoiding the figure

During the winter the Chancellor and the other Treasury ministers wil]
Teceive a host of delegations from organizations with Special interests. The
motoring organizations will come, for example, about petrol tax and the
distillers about Whisky duty. Most demands will be shrugged off with
the cynical thought that everyone is asking, to quote Lord Amory, ‘for a
reduction in one or more taxes and duties, or for an increase in one or more
items of expenditure—often both at the same time.’ But an unobtrusive
*epresentation from backbench MPs on the Government side who claim
to speak for Party opinion will haye a larger effect, especially where a
concession can be made at small cost to the Revenue. There are also more
technical Tepresentations—for example, from accountants? organization 2
on Inland Revenue Practice—which receive Very serious attention. ,

1c t that Budget security is usually more effective. Each member of

and the lingering controversies about Budget defar,

i all I can attempt here are a few background remarks,
In both its traditional role as a Tevenue raiser, and its Modern Tok
€conomic regulator, the Treasury has always been Concerned Wit
f rates. But contrary to popular belief it has had only a limiteg n
deciding what kind of taxes to levy and how they should be applied
The explanation of this little-appreciated fact is the indepe
existence of the Boards of Inland Revenue and Customs and f;
There has normally been a very small division of the Treasury y
combines a co-ordinating role on tax and budgetary Strategy together
many other duties. At the time of going to press the division in que:

was labelled Fiscal and Incomes Policy’ (already referred to on p,

and contained about nine officials, including those then labelled “execy

staff. Their job was to Oversee the practical arrangements for Bud,

Finance Bills, and other economic packages, as well as to hold the.

between the two Revenue Departments, which might have Conflic

views on the desirable development of the tax system. At the insistenc
Mr Roy Jenkins their task was broadened to include Studies of fut
Strategy and an Assistant Secretary with Inland Revenue €xperience |
brought in. But this was the merest beginning, and the resources at th
disposal could not compare with those of the Revenue Departments
whose toes they were carefully instructed Not to tread.

One tax on which officials in the division were having to make detai
decisions after 1966 was the Selective Employment Tax. This was becar
of its unusual method of collection—via the National Insurance stan
with reimbursements from the Department of Employment—which d
Not involve the Revenue Departments, Another was Policy on the vehic

excise duty, as the Ministry of Transport was only responsible for admii
istration.
One or more of the €conomic advisers may happen to be a tax speciali
(a recent example being Professor Kaldor); and the other advisers, as we
4s some of the senior officials, may well have ideas on the subject, Event
have shown how very difficult it is to steer a middle course betwee:
relying on the Professionals of the Revenue Departments (who suffer fron
the inevitable limitation of vision of all such institutions) and rushing ir
with general idea which have not been fully worked out and are full of
Practical anomalies,
The two Revenue Departments Teport independently to the Chancellor,
4 privilege of Which they are very jealous, The only advantage the most
Senior Treasury Officials have is that they are Personally closer to him. But
as the Chancellor cannot be Present at more than a tiny proportion of the
Meetings, the Treasury Tepresentative js Normally at most Drimus inter
pares,
The legal Separation of powers does not today produce any overt strains

FORWARD PROJECTION IN PRACTICE ee
ae
The chronic weakness of the public expenditure SUrVeys of the 19605 a
their tendency to underestimate future costs. The first Survey, puby ie
==) by the Conservative Government in December 1963 (but carried ou gy
= previous summer), showed public expenditure tising by an annual a, ae
=F of 4:1 per cent in real terms, It is Pretty clear that when the surye yas
= rolled forward a further year under the Conservatives in the summ, ena
== 1964 a figure well in excess of 4 per cent emerged—either as q Tesult of ney
Programmes adopted in the intervening twelve months, or as a result of
J the recosting of the earlier Programmes, On top of this were added the
. social benefits decided upon by the Labour Government in October 1, 064
= in its first days of office, a
: After reviewing the whole programme, and imposing a ceiling on the
7 defence spending, the new Government announced in February 196s a
new 44 per cent annual average limit for the growth of Public expendi.
ture for the five-year period 1964-5 to 1969-70. This was Seared to
unrealistic National Plan target rates of growth for the whole economy, ; It
would, however, be quite unfair to blame the public expenditure control
System for the weaknesses of general economic policy. Much more relevant
was the persistent failure of public expenditure to stay within even the
announced guidelines, "
During the first three years of Mr Callaghan’s Period, up to 1967-8,
public expenditure grew by an average not of 44 per cent, but of about
6 per cent. This Excess is too large to be explained simply by the phasing |
of the programme, After the announcement of the 4} Per cent ceiling, a
Series of emergency cuts in public expenditure were announced on no less _
than five Occasions—July 1965, July 1966, July 1967, November 1967, and
January 1968. Yet the expected effect of all these heroic measures was,
according to a Written Parliamentary Answer on 30 January 1968, no
more than to hold the average increase in the five years to 1969-70 to ©
just above the 44 per cent limit announced originally, an
__ It would be Wrong to assume that the Problems of the past will persist —
indefinitely into the future. Many of the changes planned by politicians
and officials eventually do happen after a long time-lag. By the turn of thom
decade public expenditure may well be rising at a lower average annual —
rate than in the Past, and perhaps give the Government in the early 19708
a real choice between Cutting taxes and increasing the public services,
Even so, the lack of relation between announced plans and realized =

OY pe VOTE ON ACCOUNT
One of the most glaring sel of expenditure Planning was long a
presentational one, This was the lack of relation between the annual Estj~
| mates presented to Parliament and the concepts used for control Purposes,
A striking example was in 1968 when, @ month after the Prime Minister
| had announced severe cuts in public expenditure, the Estimates—which
| were summarized every February in the ‘Vote on Account’—showed an
increase of over £1,000m., or more than 10 per cent for 1968-9. This
compared with a ‘true increase’ in public expenditure at Constant prices of
‘about 32 per cent’, The Leader of the Opposition was able to make
great play with the Vote on Account, and both domestic opinion and
overseas holders of sterling were totally confused, but probably feared the
worst, i
One or two parts of the official explanation of the discrepancy, such as
the offsetting of higher family allowances by higher taxation, may be open
2 duestion But by and large the Treasury’s view of the misleading nature

; Appendix: THE NATIONALIZED INDUSTRIES

: industries in the same way as the rest of the public sector. The nationalized

: the Treasury sees its relations with them as that of bankers to clients. The Hy

Sa White Paper published in November 1967, entitled ir
ized Industries: A Review of Economic and Fi enancial Objectives ( as g
3437), was a big improvement. Its starting Point was that nauga™
industries were expected to use discounted cash flow* technig es iz,
major investment proposals, which were expected to yield ar ede
cent. Particularly risky projects were naturally expected to show ag
prospective rate of return. : i ig]
The justification for an 8 per cent return was that it exceeded th, -
the Treasury had to pay on its own long-term borrowing on behalf o¢ 4.
industries and that it was also comparable with the rate of expect aoe
risk investments in the private sector, after taking into account dig o
in tax, investment grants, and other circumstances. The nationg lis
industries in practice pay little Corporation Tax and, except in the cau
steel, do not qualify for investment grants, which are confined to m anuf
turing industries. The Treasury, therefore, took a number of hypoi et
typical investments yielding the postulated 8 per cent, before either tax y
paid or grant received, and converted them into the post-tax post-grg
equivalent. Theresult ranged from 6 per cent on an investmentin the sery;
sector where no grant was payable to 14 per cent on manufacturing inve
ment receiving the maximum rate of grant in a development district. (
fact that the post-tax return in private industry should in many cases he
exceeded the pre-tax ones indicated the generosity of the investme
incentives.) The centre of the range, 10 per cent, corresponded y.
closely with ‘views expressed by industrialists’ on the minimum nd
return in large companies calculated after Corporation Tax, on a discoum
cash-flow basis. ay
Judging by its memorandum to the Select Committee on Nationaliz
Industries in 1967-8, the Treasury would like to raise the real rate
return a little. The argument is that only a very small proportion of priv:
investment is eligible for the highest grant on the whole of its costs;
simple examination of the range, therefore, gives too much value to #
extreme. A weighted average would show that the 8 per cent test yield frc

industries have, however, probably been the various ventures in prices and
‘comes policy. These brought new respectability to the old political urge

which could not easily be removed.

LABOUR’S FIRST WAGE FREEZE i
Another fact that is often forgotten is that Sir Stafford Cripps had a wage |
freeze in 1948, some thirteen years before Mr Selwyn Lloyd and eighteen 4
years before Mr Harold Wilson. In February 1948 the Treasury put out a q
Statement on Incomes, Costs, and Prices, which said, ‘There is no justifica— 2
tion for any general increase of individual money incomes,’ although there 1 ‘
might be exceptions in the case of undermanned industries, Many of the q
5 . Pp . . enhe) y . .
doctrines of that White Paper have cropped up again and again in official ‘Y
ronouncements over the subsequent twenty years, irrespective of the ‘|
P i Q q! y iy “area Pp nN
Party in office—right down to the habit of saying ‘incomes’ when the
E S ying q
writer really means wages. |
_One difference between Cripps and his later emulators was that Cripps .
did not interfere with arbitration in the public sector. Sir Stafford said in
his 1950 Budget speech that wages had been left to free negotiations and
the Government had simply put its views before the country. :
ply P 7
The TUC protested that it had not been consulted first; but, as in 1966
and in contrast to 1961, accepted, with a few qualifications, the principle
of the pause. After the devaluation of sterling in September 1949, the
TUC hee
General Council went further still and even recommended the
Suspension of cost-of-living sliding-scale agreements, an idea which it

‘BUTSKELLISM’ Mec
These attitudes of the Bank of England affected economic policy boti
under the Labour Government and under the Conseryative Gover nme
that succeeded it in October 195. it
Another link was the philosophy of ‘Butskellism’, aterm compounded by
The Economist out of the names of Butler and Gaitskell. Butskellism was
already coming to the fore in the last few years of the Labour Gove ‘nment
although its progress was interrupted for a little while by the Korean War
It was an interesting mixture of planning and freedom, based on th
economic teachings of Lord Keynes. Planning during this period was
concerned with one global total—the amount that was spent on goods and
services—normally known as ‘the level of demand’. (Subsequently. the
more precise expression ‘pressure of demand’ came into use, which is th
ratio of demand to the country’s productive capacity.) If produ tion
sagged, or unemployment looked like creeping up, extra purchasing powel
Was pumped into the System through the Budget, the banks, or the hire-
purchase houses, If employment was overfull, or the pound came unde

minds,’ he said, carrying on the theme he had begun the previous year,

In his Budget speech of April 1955, Mr Butler listed the results of
voluntary moderation. Between 1953 and 1954, output per man rose by
two and a half per cent but wages and salaries rose by about seven and a
half. In his speech on the emergency Budget of October 1955, he gave
another progress report. Wage rates had risen by five points between
| December 1954 and March 1955. Between March and June they had risen
| by another three points and since then by a further point, making nine
points in all. In December 1955 there was a Cabinet reshuffle and Mr
| Butler was replaced by Mr Macmillan. But it was not until seven years
| after munisters had first identified the problems in their speeches that a 5
Conservative Chancellor intervened to impose a wage freeze.

If one compares the line-up on floating rates in the early rgsos with the ‘ \
way in which opinion divided on the issue in the middle 1960s, one is 14
struck by the power of fashion in economic policy. In the earlier period | \
the idea that we could dispose of our balance-of-payments problem by 1%
leaving sterling to 1ts own devices was advocated by economists and a
politicians who were regarded as “right-wing? Indeed, every Conservative \ 4
Chancellor before Mr Selwyn Lloyd considered letting the pound float up ' 4
and down to find its own level in the world currency markets. If no one Wat
took the plunge it was through lack of nerve rather than doctrinal inhibi-
tions. _It was among ‘progressive’ and ‘left-of-centre’ economists that
exchange rate changes were violently opposed as a false solution that did
not get to the root of our difficulties.
Later the pendulum swung right back. Any thought of touching sterling
when it was clearly overvalued was long denounced by Press and Gi
. y *. 6 y .
commentators as irresponsibly extreme. Labour and Conservative leaders, ;
combined with the City and Bank of England to create a climate of
opinion which regarded devaluation when it came as a national disaster;
and advocates of floating rates were treated as eccentrics, remote aca-
demics, or far-out radicals (or worst of all ‘Powellites’).
oye main first-hand accounts of Robot. An account of Robot and the convertibility
rc that succeeded it can be found in The Management of the British Economy, by
a x Dow (Cambridge University Press, 1964) and Britain and the Post-war Ewro-
Payments Systems, by Graham L, Rees (University of Wales Press, 1993):

aa looking at Robot with the Controversies of the 1960s in mind a)
might have expected the expansionist section of the Treasury around Hall ‘
and Plowden to have been its strongest backers. The Teasons why it took 4
the opposite view centred not so much on the floating rate, as on the \
convertibility aspects of Robot. In 1952 the ‘dollar shortage’ was still 4
acute, and if sterling had become convertible foreign holders might well ‘
have rushed to switch their pounds into dollars at the expense of the Wh
British reserves, as happened during Dr Dalton’s ill-fated experiment in WT
the summer of 1947. On the trade side other countries might have been ay
encouraged to cut down on UK imports in order to accumulate convertible | ,
sterling for use in making dollar purchases. A floating rate in the winter of |
1951-2 might thus have meant a heavily depreciated rate, and an unneces- |
sarily severe fall in British living standards; moreover, owing to the
industrial overstrain caused by rearmament, British exports might not
have been able to respond to the stimulus that devaluation normally
brings.
These were not the only arguments used. Lord Salter’s account shows
that the more familiar notion that devaluation meant default, together with
horror at the idea of blocking the sterling balances, played a prominent part
in the opposition to ‘Robot’. Nor should it be forgotten that “progressive”
Views were often identified—quite wrongly in my opinion—with opposi-
tion to economic liberalism.
Itis questionable if the fierce hostility of the ‘progressives’ to Robot was
Well judged even in 19 52. The operation might well have led to a realign-
ment of currencies which would have put an end to the dollar shortage.
Although British living standards would temporarily have fallen, the

| THE ECONOMICS OF STOCKTON-ON-TEES ce
; rold Macmillan, who succeeded Butler at No, 17, ee
e ¥ oe regarded as an ‘inflationist’ by Treasury and Bank, ofa
i (after he became Prime Minister) by some of his own Cabinet colle
: His views were indeed coloured by his acquaintance with magg ieee
f ment at his old constituency of Stockton-on-Tees in the inter-war Plo
: and this was a subject of some mockery in Whitehall. Officials were ga
keep a mental tally of the number of times he mentioned Stockton.
Tees in any one week. But although his preoccupation with prewar d ae
sion was not a fruitful vantage point for diagnosing the trends oft
1950s, Macmillan had some wisdom on his side. For behind his Teferen,
to Stockton was a well-founded fear that prosperity and full employme
could be too easily sacrificed by a one-sided devotion to ‘sound money’
the expense of other objectives.
He resented the restricting influence of sterling on British econon
expansion, and would clearly have loved to have found a way of getting
of the sterling-area ‘bank’ which the ‘family firm’ had inherited. He y
passionately interested in the international liquidity problem, which (lo
before most Central Bankers) he rightly regarded as a matter of generati
more internationally acceptable money to finance the growth of wor
trade; and he persisted in sending American Presidents notes on t
subject. The simplest way of solving the problem, he always maintaine
was to increase the price of gold; but the fetish that the American lead
made of the existing gold—dollar parity prevented a solution along th
lines throughout his period in office.
Cynics always underestimated Macmillan’s economic understandi
He was intellectually unhappy at the need to exhort people to export mo
The occasional speech in which he recalled the vanished mechanisms of
nineteenth-century gold standard, and wondered why there was today
automatic system of bringing exports and imports into balance, again we
not the platitudes that superficial listeners supposed. He always had
hankering after a floating exchange rate, and would have introduced 0
if given the slightest encouragement.
Macmillan was, however, Tusty on the mechanics of economic cont
by the time he got to No. r1, and he had little intuitive feeling for econon
timing. Yet he often had a better understanding of the logic of the rea
major issues than either his Treasury or his Bank advisers; and it mt
never be forgotten that Macmillan was one of the very, very few Conser\
tive politicians who had been right before the war both about unemplo
ment and about the appeasement of Nazi Germany.

One little-known incident concerns the financing of the Aswan Da
(It will be recalled that the Western decision to cancel aid for this dan
precipitated Nasser’s nationalization of the Canal.) When Dulles suddenly
decided to cancel all US aid for the project, Mr Thorneycroft, who was
then President of the Board of Trade, proposed to Eden, after som
informal discussions with the other European participants, that Britain
| should go ahead with her share, even without the Americans. ‘Such a
decision might well have changed the whole course of subsequent events.
But Eden was furious at the mention of this idea and it was never properly
examined in Cabinet. i a

tHORNEYCROFT 1 AND II
Peter T horneycroft (now Lord Thorneycroft), who succeeded Mac- M
Sais at the Exchequer when the latter moved up to No. to, meh 1
compared to Mr Selwyn Lloyd; and there are certain obvious similarities, a |
Both imposed tough financial policies in the teeth of fierce opposition and \\ Va
eventually earned Mr Macmillan’s displeasure. Neither was a man of great \
cuile or subtlety; and both pursued their policies with a dogged deter- Va 4
mination, irrespective of the effect on their own political fortunes, which \ q
won admiration even from their critics. Neither could fairly be written off \ E
as a Tory reactionary (Thorneycroft was, for example, a keen European Vay |
and a free trader; Selwyn Lloyd supported the abolition of the death Ven
penalty), although both found their chief backing in the financially orthodox WW
wing of the Conservative Party. ya
Yet there were also important differences. Peter Thorneycroft never
claimed to be an intellectual high-flier; but he was rarely ata loss for words 1
and was much more interested than the average politician in general wh
economic ideas. After his resignation he contributed to a professional ae
symposium on the Radcliffe Report on the monetary system.* Selwyn Val
Lloyd, by contrast, was easily embarrassed both in public speeches and in
conversation, and was ill at ease in general economic discussion. He |
seemed to see the world (like Bertrand Russell in the early 19208) asa |
collection of unrelated atomic facts to be considered one at a time. Yet as
Chancellor he had a less misconceived idea of what was wronp with the
economy than Thorneycroft, and left more of value behind him.
Although Mr Thorneycroft was no intellectual heavyweight, he had a
| good many sound instincts. The Aswan Dam affair, just deal *
case in point; and during the Suez crisis he never took sue ,
Rie about a world tanker shortage that obsessed many other mem! ae
| © Cabinet. In the very early days of the Common Market, aroun'

i} A CHANGE OF a i
ii es ber Measures’ of 1957 marked a turning aside iz
H) is ae country had travelled under Successive 14 th
| Conservative Governments. Mr Thorneycroft’s supporters saw thi,
i than some of his critics. At the time of Mr Butler’s auturm, 1 Be
i 1955, or of the curbs imposed by Mr Macmillan in 1956, it cong P< of
! be argued that the economy was genuinely overloaded. Un mploy,.
(seasonally adjusted) was down to 1-1-2 per cent; and there w ere me
7 two recorded unfilled vacancies for every man out of work. A deflarz 10:
policy in these circumstances did not necessarily imply sacrificing
my! growth of the domestic economy for the sake of sterling, 4 aye
But by 1957 the situation was radically different. Although stij]
f unemployment had been on a rising trend for two years and ooked .
moving higher. Despite several years of rising investment, Product
had only just regained its 1955 peak. Treasury economists who. had ¢
ported the Butler and Macmillan squeezes of 1955 and 1956 saw that {
pressure of demand on supply had already eased and they expected mo,
slack to develop in the coming months. They feared that the Thorneyer,
measures would aggravate a recession that was already on the way. Ever
worse was the fact that the Thorneycroft measures were imposed ;
iene to - flight - hot money when the balance of payments itself Ag
1n comfortable surplus. lf
In December 1956, Government spokesmen said publicly that the t
Britain could hope for was a bare current balance in the year ending mi
1957. A few months later Mr Thorneycroft told Parliament that he w
looking forward to a current surplus of £125m. Speaking at the IM
meeting in September 1957, he announced that the surplus had worke
out at over £200m. and he expected a bigger surplus in the followi ;
twelve months. By deflating the economy in the face of his own figures
was proclaiming to the world that production and employment in Britai
would be held back whenever currency speculators decided to take
gamble against the Pound, or traders delayed their payments—howeve

al tragedy of 1957 is the missed opportunity on the sterli
_But the roe sterling crises, Central Bankers met the state a
ae ; as an argument against allowing the pound to float or against
tampering with the exchange rate in any way. If the pound ‘went, it was :
claimed, there would then be a run on the dollar, and this would lead to a
general flight from currencies into gold with untold consequences. This
argument carried great force with a British Cabinet which regarded the
special relationship with America as the mainstay of its foreign policy. It \
did not, however, apply at all in 1957, as the first signs of weakness in the ‘
dollar did not emerge until well on in 1958. Thus 1957 was the last sterling |
crisis when Britain could have abandoned the sterling rate fixed by Cripps h
in 1949 without any risk of rocking the international boat; and the |
opportunity was thrown away. )

It was also the last sterling crisis for more than a decade in which a |
floating rate could have been honestly presented as something different in a
conception from devaluation. For in the 1950s the pound was not yet |
decisively overvalued at $2:80. Overseas payments were in balance, taking 4
one year with another, even though the economy was on average being run i
ata high pressure of demand—considerably higher than in the 1960s. a

URING the period after the 1959 election, events moved on two el

THE TRAUMAS OF 1960 } C
The period of economic rethinking began before Mr Amory Jeft the
Exchequer—unlike Mr Thorneycroft, voluntarily, and on genuine per- |
sonal grounds. Following the credit restrictions he imposed in April, /
production stopped rising but, to the great disappointment of the Trea- ae
sury, there were few compensating benefits in other directions. This \
simple episode had a traumatic effect on Treasury thinking. As the j
months went by, it stimulated three main lines of reflection, which were |
to be the source of all that followed: 1
1. After all the investment of the past few years ‘either production
should have increased or employment decreased,’ as Sir Robert Hall
put it in an article in The Economist after he retired.* In the event |
the curbs on demand stopped production from rising, but did not
ag any unemployment or even stop the increase in the labour \
orce, |
Later research, put in hand partly as result of such episodes, made .
this paradox easier to explain, It appears that in both recessions and |
booms the movement of employment normally lags behind produc- i
ton. At the top of the boom the labour force is usually not yet fully 1
adjusted to the level of production; so employment may go on _ ,
expanding for some months after output has stopped rising. Con- |
versely employment can go on falling even after the bottom of a mE \

THE NEW LOOK FOR STATE INDUSTRIES : ;
Another rather different product of the economic rethinking of Ig60 was .
the approach to the nationalized industries. A great many of the changes
since 1960 have, of course, been due to developments in the nationalized
industries themselves; all the same, the Treasury has never received the |
praise due to it for its undoubted share in the transformation.* The new |
policies were heralded in July 1961 by a White Paper, The Financial and ,
Economic Obligations of the Nationalized Industries, the very title of which ;
. . oe bac )

Se kee concept which first came into vogue in the 1960 reappraisal .
was the payroll tax. Two different ideas, with different histories, were
involved here, although this was not sufficiently appreciated at the time. /
One was a ‘surcharge’ on payrolls as a temporary economic regulator to s
stimulate or restrain private spending. The other was for a payroll tax as .
a permanent part of the tax system, which would not add to the tax
burden, but replace other imposts. i
The latter idea arose from a study of the supposed structural weaknesses
of the British economy. Sir Robert Hall’s disappointment, expressed in his |
Economist article, that the 1960-1 investment boom had had so little effect 1
on productivity, has already been mentioned. In the short run, he attri- 7
buted it to labour hoarding. But he also diagnosed deeper defects. A great }
deal of new investment, he Suggested, had been of the wrong kind. Too i]
much had taken the form of simple additions to productive capacity and /
too little took the form of modernization or new processes. As ' result i
there was not enough labour to man the new plant and equipment; and in i
i hen there ‘ea
* time of boom, overfull employment of labour was reached wi ded |
Was still a great deal of unutilized industrial capacity. Sir Robert conclu :
that the labour hoarding should be discouraged and investment given |

THE 1961 STERLING CRISIS
All the new thinking about Britain’s industrial structure and on economic |
regulators was taking place against a gathering sterling crisis, which was ,
itself handled by traditional stop-go methods. This originated, like every- .

payments deficit for a whole decade, although this fact was not then \

except to those affected. Much more relevant is that it takes the public—
and even more the organs of opinion—a long period to get used to new 1
ideas, after which they become second nature. By the time of the devalua- :
ton winter of 1967-8, the Press was positively clamouring for a pay freeze 1
which the Government was reluctant to impose. |

fe Mr Lloyd introduced the 1962 Budget, the Government was not

I woes Budget would contain none of these three things and that the
ae innovation would be a tax on confectionery and ice-cream,
o ae 1962 Budget judgment is one that has ever since been regarded
inside the Treasury with embarrassment. So far from foreseeing the
: unemployment that was to come, the Chancellor in his Budget speech
actually worried that total spending would make ‘too great a call on our
resources’. The buoyant forecast was mainly due toan extremely optimistic
view of exports, which emanated from the Board of Trade. The Treasury
‘ in its own analysis of what went wrong put most stress on the surprise it }
received when, contrary to expectations, exports levelled out and then fell
: slightly after the middle of the year. As a secondary factor it blamed the \
fall in private investment, which was much steeper than the Treasury had 1
thought it would be. These two setbacks helped to generate a typical :
deflationary cycle, which led to lower employment, shorter hours, and a
fall in industrial stock building. /
An important contributory factor at the political level was that Mr |
Selwyn Lloyd was led to think that success in the Common Market i
Negotiations would be apparent by July 1962, and that an agreement |
would be on the table by September. This news, he envisaged, would ;
stimulate an investment boom. On a more intellectual level the trouble Wh
was that the Treasury economists overestimated the growth of demand /

THE DEPARTURE OF SELWYN LLOYD
The reasons for Lloyd’s dismissal have never been adequately explained.
It would be misleading to report that it was just because he was too late
and too timid in switching over to re-expansion. Most of the evidence
' suggests that any such feeling was only one element in a much more
general belief that Macmillan had gradually acquired that Lloyd was no
longer the right man for the Treasury and had become a political liability. q
The dismissal came not very long after Orpington and a whole series of a
really shattering Conservative defeats. :
‘The unfairest of reasons given for Lloyd’s dismissal, but one that is all |
too likely to be important, is the two words ‘pay pause’. Lloyd paid the | |
price for an unpopular policy which quite a few eminent men of a Labour :
and trade-union background privately believed to have been essential— i
saved the nation? was one, albeit exaggerated, remark actually made, \
Another factor was that Lloyd could not take part to Macmillan’s iv
satisfaction in ruminative general economic conversation. On complex +
Treas matters Lloyd seemed to the Premier and to some of his col- \
“agues to be too dependent on his brief, and to look at a loss when unex- i.
pie questions turned up. Whether these criticisms did justice to Lloyd : :
the Excindt or not, they were perfectly predictable when he was moved to i)
hovers equer after five years as Foreign Secretary. This dea :
May ne aggravated in Mr Macmillan’s eyes by the Wall Street ms 1
had Ae 2, and renewed talk of the difficulties of the dollar. These event :
ved the Prime Minister’s concern with the liquidity problem, i.e. ,
