rr the arrival of Reginald Maudling at the Exchequer on 13 July
Woe, we are no longer in the region of history, but of controversies
which are likely to remain current for some time to come.
Two opposed views are often put forward about his two-year Teign. One y
js that by embarking on a profligate growth Policy, he created the severe
balance-of-payments problem which was Tesponsible for most of Britain’s :
trouble. This was the ‘legacy’ view assiduously canvassed by Labour
spokesmen after the 1964 election; but those who believed it most strongly ;
and sincerely were the financial conservatives in the City of London and i
elsewhere. The opposite view, which became more fashionable after the )
failure of Labour’s own early economic policies, was that Maudling had |
embarked on a courageous expansionist experiment designed to break out 1 |
of the stop-go cycle, which might well have Succeeded—and without {
devaluation—if only the incoming Labour Ministers had not shattered
confidence in sterling, }
Both views seem to me a great deal too simpliste. But before embarking ;
on this controyersy it would be best to begin with some of the main events |
of Maudling’s tenure of the Exchequer. Unlike Mr Selwyn Lloyd, he had
never been personally close to Mr Macmillan. The Prime Minister brought i\)
him in at a low point in Conservative fortunes, when he wanted, above all,
Someone who had some notion of economic policy and was able to express 4
himself on the subject in public, Mr Maudling had these qualities. He did |
not find the intellectual framework within which policy is discussed by ]
Treasury economists and outside commentators as incomprehensible as ]
Some of his predecessors, At his best Maudling would give birth to a flow 1|
of ideas in the hope that if his own turned out to be impracticable they WD
Would at least provoke his advisers to think of preferable alternatives. His 11)
speed of work and his casual conversational manner sometimes gave a
false impression of laziness. ; 1 i
Maudling was relatively independent of his officials, because he could Mh
Tapidly absorb and even anticipate their advice. But he had too equable a |
temperament to bea ruthless administrator steamrollering his way through vi

HE verdict reached in an earlier chapter on Mr Selwyn Lloyd's ]
Tce at the Treasury was that his management of the economy was /
undistinguished, but that he deserves to be remembered for the changes he :
inaugurated in the long-term environment in which that Management
takes place. The same can be repeated fortissimo of the 1964 Labour
Government in its first three years in office.
| The Bank of International Settlements, in an analytical summary of the 1
events that led up to the 1967 devaluation, remarks; ‘It is likely that
sterling was an overvalued currency by the beginning of the 1960s and
that the situation had remained tenable only because of the improvement ;
in the terms of trade,”* Nor were such opinions reached only with hind-
sight. Mr Callaghan confessed to the House of Commons that a month
after taking office on 16 October 1964 he was advised by a ‘very senior :
monetary authority in Europe’} that sterling was in fundamental dis- ‘
equilibrium and ought to be devalued by 10~I5 per cent. It subsequently !
turned out that the authority in question was no less than Emile van
Lennep, the chairman of the EEC Monetary Committee and of OECD's |
key ‘Working Party Three’. He was accompanied by Thorkil Kristensen,
the OECD's Secretary-General, a

LABOUR’S ECONOMIC PHILOSOPHY
To ascribe everything that went rong early to lack of courage, of to
isguided political manoeuvring, would be a gross oversimplifcation.
Me Wilson and some of his leading colleagues sincerely and profoundly
relieved that a direct ‘physical’ attack on the economy's weaknesses could
succeed where ‘financial tinkering” had failed. This belief was compounded.
several elements. There was (and still is) a strong current of opinion,
represented in Conservative as well as Labour circles, which was impatient
of the Keynesian management of demand and the balance of payments and
saw ‘more fundamental’ structural reform as an alternative. To this was
sdsed a strong bias against the global approach in favour of an industry=
by-industry and firm-by-firm attack. Thirdly there was a preference for
direct ‘physical’ methods over financial forms of regulation. Interestingly
cnough, apart from a few highly emotive areas such as housing and rents,
the Labour leaders of the mid-1960s had no great liking for controls a5
such; they believed that once ‘theology’ was cast aside, industrial and trae
‘union leaders would be prepared to co-operate on a commonsense bass.
There were certain contradictions in this approach from the besianing.
Much of Labour's appeal to some of the younger members ofthe mani=
Serial classes in 1964 was based on the belief that it was on their side
‘sainst “boardroom deadwood’, Yet Labour leaders were still deeply

yes ne rumbling about the surcharge in GATT and EFTA,
Despite ia prevailed until the autumn Budget of 11 November. The
relative a Budget was t0 offset the potential inflationary effects of the
aim “ P we pensions and other benefits, and the abolition of Health
nee charges, also announced by Mr Callaghan in his Budget Speech.
in crude revenue terms the Budget was more than enough for the purpose.
The increase of 6d in the standard rate of income tax, combined with the
higher petrol duty, was expected to yield {215m. mn a full year—more than
the Exchequet would have to find for the new benefits.*
The economic arithmetic was, however, very different. The increase in
benefits, as the National Institute pointed out at the time, was almost
entirely reflected in consume spending. The increase im income tax
(which was not extended to the reduced rates) was to 2 considerable extent
at the expense of personal and company savings. The higher employers
National Insurance contributions could only be expected to reduce
demand indirectly and belatedly, as they worked through into price
increases. It would, however, be equally wrong to go to the other extreme.
The November 1964 Budget, together with the surcharge and the social
service changes, was intended to be neutral in economic terms. If it in fact
turned out inflationary, it was so by only @ «mall margin and could have
sae i a minor role in the overheating of the economy in 1965 and
early 1966. :
It was not any such fine economic arithmetic that upset the financial
markets, The feature of the Budget that caused the greatest outcry was Mr
Callaghan’s foreshadowing of his proposals for Corporation and Capital |
Gains Tax, due to be revealed in full the following April. The uncertainty
created was transmitted to the foreign exchange market, both directly and
via the gilt-edged market, pee
This episode has sometimes been used as an argument against any

Weg Chequets Ministerial meeting over the weekend of 21-2 November,
es {ly called to discuss the defence review, had been attended by Lord
ae ue well as the service chiefs; and on the following Monday Bank
Rate was raised from 5 07 per cent. Yet, instead of slowing down, the
flight from the pound accelerated, and on the Tuesday and Wednesday,
gales of sterling were 1 the Bank of England’s words, ‘massive and
growing « ie : :
Some City critics attributed the perverse reaction to the fact that Bank
Rate had been raised on @ Monday instead of the normal Thursday—
which the market, not unreasonably, regarded as a sign of panic. Others,
going a little deeper, already spotted the Government's growing habit of
talking with two different voices to two different audiences. While the
Bank Rate increase was presented to overseas and financial opinion as @
sign of the Government’s readiness to take harsh measures, it was pre-
sented at home as a technical adjustment, which was not intended to
deflate demand. Indeed, special arrangements were made to insulate local
authorities from the higher borrowing costs. The run on the pound was
due to the very natural desire of holders of sterling to protect themselves
against the threat of devaluation; and signs of Government panic only
increased their fears.
On 25 November, there was @ dramatic announcement from the Bank of
England that eleven central banks, together with the Bank for Inter-
national Settlements and the US Export-Import Bank, had arranged 2
further $3,000m, of short-term credits which Britain could draw upol ‘ap
protect the pound, The exercise seems tO have been a combined operation,
arranged almost overnight by the US and British monetary authorities.
Even this unprecedented credit did no mek” than slow down the outflow
of funds from London, The Bank of England stated that throughout
December? there was ‘a very heavy demand for foreign exchange for

encourage followed in later economic packages. After July 1965, for

Such actions admittedly carried with them a threat to the growth of
‘avestment income from abroad. The continuing annual gain to the balance
of payments, including both profits and induced exports, for every £100 of
direct overseas investment worked out according to the Reddaway Report
on average at only about £9, assuming that it was financed entirely from
the reserves.* (The greater the resort to local borrowing the greater is the
balance-of-payments return for Britain.) Saving the £100 on the reserves
here and now was regarded as more important, S long as the balance of
payments remained in fundamental disequilibrium.
Altogether the Labour Government showed itself more ready to take
disect action on the balance of payments than its predecessors. Apart
from the capital account and the export rebate already mentioned, the
growth of Government overseas spending was brought to @ halt in 1966,
after which it fell slightly. There were also a number of minor measures,
which together must have had a marginal effect, such as the increased
sums for export promotion, including subsidies for trade. missions
and industry-wide market research, and improvements in export credit.
The original Selective Employment Tax contained a Very small m- |
built export subsidy; and the investment grants which replaced the old }
allowances were deliberately concentrated on manufacturing industries, y

In any case, 08 27 July the Chancellor announced what was intended to
be the severest package of restrictions SO far introduced by the Labour

starting dates on some public-sector capital projects were postponed for

together with other restrictions made in local-authority and Government
investment, were supposed to amount to £200m. ina full year. In addition
building licences were introduced for certain private projects.
It was as a result of the July 1965 package—@ full year before the more
famous 1966 measures—that many close students became finally con-
vinced that the Government had abandoned its growth and employment
objectives, and would be prepared to deflate to the extent necessary to
maintain the exchange rate. Thus the National Plan was written off by
many people two months before it was published. The National Institute,
in its August 1965 issue, forecast that unemployment would rise to 2-2°5
per cent by the end of 1966 and that there would also be, by that ume,
small balance-of-payments surplus ‘at the cost of slowing down the rise 2
national output nearly to a stop - Things did not in practice work out that

The result was dual system of economic control with the Department 1
AG Economic Affairs and the Treasury enjoying overlapping responsibility 1
er a wide field. Thanks to the energy of Mr George Brown, and to the 1
ae that reigned among the staff in the early days, the DEA did a much

situation. Established Civil Servants were forced to re-examine many of
their long-held assumptions and many of them were—m retrospect at
Jeastglad of the experience. The DEA under Mr Brown was the least
politically doctrinaire of departments; 1t was the first to appoint ‘industrial
advisers’ seconded from senior management posts, and it was normally the
spokesman for industry, when industrial needs conflicted with other
objectives of policy. Yet it would be wrong to disguise my view that these
aims would have been better achieved without the catastrophic experiment \
of two rival economic ministers locked together in uncreative tension, with |
the Prime Minister swinging sometimes one way and sometimes the other |
on no known principle. \
The division of responsibility eventually between the two departments }
laid down in a ‘Concordat’ between them, was supposed to be that (a) the yy
DEA would concentrate on the long term and the Treasury on the short |
term; and (b) the DEA would concentrate on ‘physical resources’ and the |
Treasury on finance. “ ih
_ The distinction rested on a combination of conservative pre-Keynesian \ |
illusions about the autonomy of finance, and equally old-fashioned socialist
ideas about the physical control of industry. This was apparent well before 1
the 1964 election, when the Wilsonian form of the idea was first floated. \

in the same direction as the Production or Economics Ministry favoutes

THE DEA’S WORK
The third DEA activity was industrial policy. In the early period this was
based on a sector-by-sector search for ways of improving efficiency and \
‘nternational competitiveness. The approach soon became known to
initiates as ‘Catherwoodery’ after Mr Fred Catherwood, a former mana- ’
ging director of British ‘Aluminium, who became the first Industrial p
Director of the DEA, and later Director General of NEDC. The limitation 1
of this approach in its early stages was that it worked mainly through }
‘Little Neddies’—part-time voluntary committees, which had neither \
direct powers over the industries concerned nor incentives to offer them. 1
The market economy does not work so perfectly that the ear-stroking Wh,
activities of the ‘Little Neddies’ can be ruled out of court @ prior, and ona
long view some of these committees have played a useful part. The Vi,
Government’s mistake was to exaggerate both the magnitude of their Vi
influence and the speed with which it could take effect. | |
The fourth main field of DEA activity was in regional policy. Here @ \
large part of the work in the first year or so Was organizational, in particular | |
the setting up of Regional Boards of Government officials and Regional 1
Councils of nominated representatives. The new bodies were inevitably @
stop-gap, which could not be a substitute for the reform of local govern- 1
ment and the creation of elected regional authorities. Y i
Indeed, the DEA’s most valuable contribution to regional policy lay \
not so much in the work for which it was directly responsible, Liseaes ae
role as a central co-ordinating department in the general evolution e

138 arly SET was 4 rough-and-ready tax method of spreading the
but den—it actually increased the spread between services and those goods
not subject to tax at all. If fresh peas are served in a restaurant, the wea
pelling and washing is subject to tax. If the restaurant buys tinned peas
tg work has already been done by the manufacturer, who pays no such 7
tax. On balance—and very much on balance—this aspect of the tax was
one which could be supported by economic liberals as a way of reducing
tax discrimination between the different sectors of the economy.* Whether
on balance SET increased or decreased discrimination in the tax system
depends on whether retail distribution 1s regarded as already taxed by ,
imposts such as purchase tax. If they are, then SET simply removed the |
bias in favour of personal services such as hairdressing, laundry, banking |
and insurance, OF professional services. The discrimination in favour of |
food, transport and miscellaneous untaxed manufactured products then |
stood out all the more; and SET created new borderline discriminations |
such as the peas example quoted, or Lady Hall’s example of employees Dh
filling vending machines. In their case tax is paid if they work for special- |
ized vending firms, but not if they work on the premises of the manufac- |
turers. y 1
Supporters of SET would argue, however, that retail distribution, |
which accounts for about a tenth of the working population, was not \
hitherto subject to tax in an economically significant way. For, before SET, \
the amount of tax paid did not vary whether a product was purchased at | |
Harrods, with a great deal of personal service attached to it, oF whether it \ |
was picked up in a self-service discount store. The result was that retail Wh
services received an artificial boost and took up more manpowe than if \ 1
they had paid a pro-rata share of the indirect tax burden. Ultimately, a“ \
is up against the problem of how to assess @ tax change which increased ve | i)
differential between some of the thousands of relevant categories am |
decreased the differential between others. : |

OWN Ce few days of the Bud
e began within a few days of the Budget. ;
indeed, trout IME team to London in early May for ie oe pms de
the a "ong, It soon became known that it took an extremel oe : ;
consultations: inne els | 'y sceptical
«wy of the likelihood of reaching the ancellor s target of a payments
i ce by the end of 1966, and its scepticism was widely shared in
patel economic circles. The really heavy blow to sterling came with the
scamen’s strike, which began on 16 May and lasted six and a half weeks.
The strike reflected the Government's determination to encourage em-
ployers to resist wage claims in defiance of its incomes policy. But in select-
ing for a showdown the industry that most immediately and directly
affected the whole of Britain's trade, the Government had not chosen its
ground well and the overseas holders of sterling were the opposite of
impressed. When a Government is reduced to guessing which kind of
‘toughness’ will make the most psychological appeal to half-informed
observers abroad, it is a sign that the currency is fundamentally weak and |
more fundamental treatment is required. |
The publication of the May reserve figure, which did not conceal the ti
whole of the true loss, further upset the foreign exchange market. The \
devaluation of the Indian rupee at this time stimulated the imagination of 1]
many nearer home. Even the announcement from Basle on 13 June of yh
new swap facilities designed to offset fluctuations in the sterling balances, yl
failed to help for more than a few days. The pressure on sterling was \
aggravated by the extreme tightness of money in New York. This ]
prompted London-based banks, who had previously converted dollar \
| deposits into sterling for loans to British local authorities, to switch them
back into dollars for use in the US. Foreign exchange funds, which had |
been attracted to London by high interest rates and had helped to finance Lh
earlier payments deficits, now went back across the Atlantic. A sign of the |
international money squeeze was the increase in Bank Rates throughout |
Western Europe. But the large size of the movement out of Britain |
reflected a loss of confidence in sterling as well as the attractions of the US i
money market, on :
Mr Wilson later made much of these overseas pressures in defending his
July measures, and tried to make the TUC’s blood curdle with talk of
| another 1931”, But if the flight from sterling simply reflected international
| financial movements, rather than the British balance of payments, what
| was the relevance of deflation in Britain? The analogy with 1931 has been '

the ail ingredients of the package were the 10 per cent ‘regulator’
: charge on consumer taxes, a quite severe tightening of hire-purchase
Bur dics {1 som. of ‘cuts im public expenditure for 1967-8, and intensified
omiding controls. These were estimated ‘to reduce demand on the
domestic economy by more than £500m.’ As usual, there was no indica-
Fon of how this figure was defined or the period to which it applied. In
addition there were to be a six-month statutory freeze covering wages,
salaries and dividends, and a further six months of ‘severe restraint’.
Prices were also to be frozen, except to the extent that they reflected
‘ncreases in taxes OF import costs which could not be absorbed. The
machinery of the freeze was simply to add reserve powers of actual control
for 12 months to the ‘early-warning Bill’, then going through Parliament,
| which had originated in the crisis of the summer before.
| The measures to reduce demand were prepared by the Treasury in the
| normal way, making use of the standby packages which that Department
often has ready for occasions of this kind. At the time the Press made
ereat play with the fact that three knights—Sir Burke Trend, the Cabinet
Secretary, Sir William Armstrong and Sir Eric Roll—were told to wield
the hatchet over the weekend while Wilson was away in Moscow. This
three-man operation appeared to be mainly concerned with the public- )
expenditure side of the package, which in the end proved the opposite of
effective, as real public expenditure rose by 7 per cent in 1967-8. |
The package also contained a few politically motivated. additions with ,
negligible economic impact, designed to show that the better-off were
being hit. One of these was the 10 per cent surcharge on surtax for one |
year. Characteristically, it was a tax which hit earned income rather than
capital. At the upper end of the range some taxpayers actually paid more
than 20s in the pound for the year in question. The other measure of this
kind was the £50 Travel Allowance, which was imposed more for psycho- \
logical reasons than for its effect on the balance of payments. For the sake |
of a pseudo-egalitarian gesture, one of the most important ingredients 1m }
personal liberty was removed, leaving Britain the only country mie
Western world with comparable restrictions on travel. Yet so ek pee
was the Conservative leadership to support the $2-8o exchange ne
costs, and still at the time so fearful of Wilson's tongue-lashing sh :
anything that could be misrepresented as being ‘pro-rich’, that it eT
divide the House on the measure. Nor indeed did it challenge the logic 0

area on trade generally. Ministers were unwilling to take heed of their

A oking at the recovery in sterling, the better trade figures, and the
standstill in the wages index, they were convinced that the corner had been
turned. It was (us that they worried about the increase in unemployment.
But the 2 per cent level it reached in the first quarter of 1967 was a good
deal less than some of the more scarifying talk the previous July had
suggested. The alarmists had forgotten that the danger of unemployment
getting out of hand is usually greatest not one, but two winters after a
Government has taken severe deflationary action.
In more sophisticated form the optimism even affected the official
forecasters. In his Budget speech Mr Callaghan predicted ‘a surplus in
1967 as a whole with an even bigger one in 1968.’ In fact he expected to be
just on the right side in 1967 and to achieve a £200m. surplus in 1968.
These forecasts did not seem unreasonable to independent experts, such
as those at the National Institute. Previous experience had always sug-
gested that sufficiently determined deflationary action could secure @
temporary breathing space. The real payments trouble had im the past
come after re-expansion had been under way for some while. Few of the
Government’s economic critics thought that the balance of payments
would be in deficit the year after the severest deflationary measures ever
inflicted in the postwar period. The warning they did give was 2 more
general one: as a result of the deficits and debts of the previous three oF
four years the Government was walking a tightrope, and could not afford .
deviation on the wrong side of the central forecast, even well within the 1
range of error of the exercise. The part of the official machine that seems
to have carried balance-of-payments optimism t the furthest extreme Was |
the Bank of England. This was hardly surprising in view of its enthusiasm |
for the July measures, The Bank had a natural desire to show that s oo "i
was not in fundamental disequilibrium; and like all human insutul a })\
tended to pay more attention to those straws in the wind which a camabes
its belief than to those which did not, ‘The March #997 Pe
prematurely lyrical about the benefits of an ‘easter supply post .

\ The 1967 Budget was presented by the Chancellor as a neutral one

more than both the Government’s long-term public expenditure target of
4:25 per cent per annum and the estimated underlying rate of economic
growth of 3 per cent. The Treasury was, of course, aware that the net
effects of its policy were expansionary. Indeed public investment had been

investment. But the department had no wish to broadcast this fact to the
| foreign exchange market. ‘As it was, the figures for Government spending

public expenditure ‘cuts’ in general. tet

THE BEGINNING OF THE END
Meanwhile, despite the large increase in public expenditure, it became
clear that the economy was not expanding by the 3 per cent forecast by
the Chancellor. Partly for this reason and partly because productivity was,
for the time being at least, expanding faster than in the past, seasonally Bh
adjusted unemployment was creeping upwards from the 2 per cent level it
had already reached before the Budget. A sinister feature was that the most |
important single reason for the slow growth of output was that exports
levelled off in the first quarter and then started falling. Imports, moreover,
quickly recovered from their artificial dip before the end of the surcharge.
Visible trade was again in deficit in the first quarter of 1967 and, in the
second quarter, the trade gap had widened to an average of nearly £50m- i
4 month, even before the Middle East War had had any real impact on the | |
gures. i
Nevertheless, confidence in sterling held up until the middle of May. ti}, |
Short-term funds continued to return to London; there was reduction in
Bank Rate from 6 to 5*5 per cent as late as 4 May, and during the month hI
as a whole there would have been a small rise in the reserves had it not ih
been for a repayment to the IMF. The sharp turnround im the sterling
market occurred, in fact, in the middle of May—about three weeks before
the Arab-Israeli war, Although the increase in tension in the Middle Pe
and in other parts of the world contributed, these were probably not the !

‘THE CENTRE CANNOT HOLD’
Until the summer of 1967 it would have been incorrect to have spoken of
a ‘Treasury view on devaluation. The Department accepted that Minis-
ters had ruled it out altogether, and that it was up to officials to advise on
the implications of this decision. Naturally the degree of enthusiasm or
reservation towards this basic decision varied from person to person.
(There was probably a modest correlation between age and attitudes to
this question, as there was among outside opinion.) It was, however,
possible to identify a more definite view among a central core of ‘regular’
economists and officials concerned with economic policy, which was
already in existence under the Conservatives in the early 1960s and
persisted right up to 1967. This was, very briefly, that it was not worth the
risks and dislocation of a devaluation, if the parity could be preserved by
running the economy with a margin of up to about 2 pet cent unemployed
but it was not desirable to go much above this level for long just for the
sake of the exchange rate. This body of opinion was important, beaut x
stood in the middle between those who had long been advocating devalua- I
tion and those who saw it as a dishonout OF disaster which 1t would be
worth paying a very high price to avoid, By August of September, ee V
members of this central school of policy-planners had switched sides me \
hee to the conclusion that the $2°80 parity could not be saved at Vi
moderate price in wnem loyment. Wh
The Commonwealth aed Ministers were informed at the on )
annual meeting in the third week of September that there would be an

of it in the context of the emergency plans that must always exist some-

Gunes Finance Ministers and central banks had also been engaged
in similar contingency planning, and discussions had been held in |
organizations such as the EEC’s Monetary Committee and (informally
and privately) among the topmost OECD economic staff. Anyone who
had travelled at all in Continental financial centres would have known
that a devaluation of rg per cent had long been regarded as tolerable and
not one to be followed en bloc by the rest of Europe. Whether or not this
figure would have been the British Treasury’s first choice, it seemed from
its initial over-optimistic pre-devaluation calculations to give a sufficient
margin of safety, if given time to work. }
The die was finally cast when the final version of the autumn economic ;
forecasts became available at the end of October or beginning of Novem-
ber. Not only was a £500m. deficit correctly forecast for 1967, but a }
figure of not much less was forecast for 1968, despite the prospective
improvement in world trade and the gradual return to normal in the oil
and freight markets after the Middle Eastern War, and there was no real
reason to be confident of a surplus even in 1969. With unemployment
already 2:3 per cent, the domestic cost of using deflation alone would
have been prohibitive; and it was no part of the philosophy of the domin-
ant middle-of-the-road wing of the Treasury to make the attempt. At
the end of the day, the Bank Governor, Sir Leslie O’Brien, loyally }
accepted the decision and the nation was spared a dramatic resignation |
or calls for a national Government to save sterling from this particular é \
source. |

F That evening the Prime Minister omitted his customary lion-headed

{ On Thursday morning, 16 November, the British Cabinet approved
; the devaluation of the pound. The previous night the BBC had broadcast
| a Continental rumour of a possible fresh $1,000m. credit for Britain. The
Hl report was perhaps am echo of the proposal that M. Schweitzer had
y briefly considered and rejected. Such reports could have been helpful
to the British authorities, because the implication that there was an

sterling, But an immediate problem facing the Cabinet was that Mr
Robert Sheldon, the Chairman of the Labour backbench Economic
Committee, had tabled a Private Notice Question to the Chancellor about
the reports of fresh credits, which the Speaker had accepted. The Question
could have been stopped by giving either the Speaker or Mr Sheldon
(who was a staunch devaluationist) a hint. The failure to do so was yet
another example of the price paid for the obsession with secrecy in the
governmental process. In the end Mr Callaghan refused either to confirm
or deny the reports from Paris and advised members not to believe ;
everything they read in the Press.

widely disliked abroad, which Whitehall thought it discreet to jettison
now that front-door devaluation had been adopted. The increase in
Corporation Tax from 40 to 42% per cent was not expected to restrict
demand, but was mainly a political move to trim the increase in profits
expected to follow from devaluation. The whole package was 2 good
deal less than had originally been put up to Ministers.
‘THE POUND IN YOUR POCKET’ |
Was this a forced devaluation? In the technical sense, no. Apart from the
foreign-market fiasco on the Friday, the operation was well planned and
well carried out. For the first time the devaluation of a reserve currency
had been internationally agreed and accepted beforehand. Only 2 handful
of countries followed; and many of the major sterling-area countries, |
including Australia or South ‘Africa, did not follow the British pound |
downwards. For this reason the devaluation—although it was not realised
at the time—was probably larger than the 1949 move from $4 t0 $2°80, )
when most of the rest of the world devalued as well. ani |
But in any except a technical sense, the move was forced on the Govern- |
ment in flat contradiction of its intended strateey and at 2 particularly
bad time both nationally and internationally. (While July 1906 was the
best time to have devalued psychologically, early 1967 would have ey | |
the best time economically.) Mr Callaghan behaved with dignity, aie |
that devaluation had been a defeat, and his letter of resignation was e . |
Prime Minister’s hands by the time of the announcement. Having mee | |

The Pattern of Economic }
Policy

e, Reserves and Living Standards O ‘Underlying |
WT O Misplaced Fundamentalism Oo The! Bolanes of |
Payments CD) Overseas Spending O Britain’s Competitive ]
Position 0 Sterling’s International Role D Was Policy tl

any people have felt that behind the twists and turns of British t

Moeconone policy in the 1950s and 1960s there must have been a tT
common pattern, Similar episodes appeared to recur far too frequently to ]
be the product of chance, and certain forces appeared to be at work for ]
long periods at a time. |

Although patterns, like beauty, exist in the eye of the beholder, they are |
still worth tracing. An analysis of the period in terms of a few fairly simple
economic and political forces not only helps to make sense of what other-
wise would be a confused succession of crises and controversies; it also ]
sheds some light on why Britain’s experience differed from other countries’ |
and may even give a few clues to the prospects for the 1970s. e}

The best point of departure is to look at the record from the time the
British economy moved back to a normal basis after the Korean War to
the 1967 devaluation. Table 15 is a conventional ‘league table’ comparing ; |
the growth of national income per person employed in several countries. it
A little later on in this chapter I am going to suggest that a close examina-
tion of this table provides, in conjunction with other considerations, |
grounds for moderate optimism; that the sterling crises of the 1960s
concealed an underlying improvement; and that, given a little luck, Britain
should be able to emerge by the 1970s from her Cinderella position in the
growth stakes, |

None of this can conceal the historical fact that Britain was near the
bottom of the growth stakes over the whole of the period 1950-67. The
USA, which came below Britain in the table, had of course a much higher
absolute level of income per head from the beginning. Comparisons of
absolute levels of output are subject to even greater statistical uncertainties
than rates of growth. The available estimates do suggest, however, that
both output and consumption per head of population, in several Con-
tinental countries, overtook Britain some time in the early 1960s.

and 1959 (when the Conservatives won the third successive election

per cent and personal disposable incomes by 28 per cent in real terms.
The two main reasons for the disproportion were the fall in defence
spending and the improvement in the terms of trade. When the Con-
servatives came to office in the early 1950s they inherited the rearmament
programme which the postwar Labour Government had embarked upon
\ at the time of Korea. This programme, which the Conservatives reduced,
} reached its peak in 1953; in the following seven years defence spending
actually dropped by a quarter (allowing for changes in prices) and fell even
more as a proportion of the National Product. The declining burden of
defence spending was probably the main reason why taxation could fall
as a proportion of the national income for so many years runing.
. In addition the country enjoyed a windfall from the terms of trade.

‘UNDERLYING’ PERFORMANCE
for the bare record of the 1950s and 1960s. To
oo cee shaped policy it is essential to distinguish ae
iying movements and temporary fluctuations, both in output and in the
palance of payments. Several synonyms are often used for the underlying
rate of growth, such as ‘growth of productive capacity’, ‘growth of
productive potential’, or, more tendentiously, ‘maximum rate of sustain-
able growth’ per ath
‘The main point can be illustrated by imagining that output rises by 6
per cent in the course of a particular year. At the end of that year,
unemployment has gone down, overtime working has increased and
industrialists report that they are working nearer their potential limits. It
js then useful to divide the 6 per cent increase into two parts. One part
represents simply the absorption of slack and cannot go on once the
) economy is working at capacity. The other part represents the increase in
productive potential or capacity—due to increased efficiency, better 1
machinery, the increase in the labour force (if there is one) and countless
other factors.
The ‘rate of growth of productive capacity’ can thus best be defined as
the rate of growth of national output that could be maintained without
any change in the pressure of demand as measured by unemployment and
related indicators. If, in the year of our example, output had risen by only
3 per cent, if the unemployment percentage had been the same at the
beginning and at the end, and there were no reason to attribute this to a |
time-lag in recruiting or dismissing workers, then productive capacity
could be said to have been growing at 3 per cent. Output would have been
growing at a ‘normal’ rate and the “pressure of demand’ would have been
constant.
It is convenient to divide the growth of productive potential itself into
the underlying growth of output per man-year (‘underlying productivity’)
and the underlying growth of the labour force. For example, in the
period 1961-5, the official estimate for the growth of productive capacity
was an average of 3-3 per cent per annum, of which about 26 represented
the underlying increase in productivity and 0-7 the growth of the labour
force.* In subsequent years little or no growth was expected in the labour
force and an actual dip was expected in the early 1970s. ve :
There are many complications about measuring both productive
capacity and underlying productivity in practice. ‘An allowance must be
made for the time-lag between changes in business conditions and the
ensuing changes in employment. Other adjustments have to be made for
* DEA Progress Report, June 1967- PeeevonnH

main question was whether the balance of payments and the state of world |
trade would allow a reasonably full employment to be maintained, and a
| faster growth of productive potential to be translated into actual output. \
THE BALANCE OF PAYMENTS i alia ieee |
In the case of the balance of payments it is even more important than when : ;
discussing the growth of output to try to separate year-to-year vagaries vu
from the underlying state of affairs. The object is to remove from 4
particular year’s figures, as far as possible, abnormal elements, due to, SY.
an unusually large increase in stockbuilding, a once-for-alll long-term he
or a Middle Eastern war. This should provide some idea of what a
balance of payments would have been if the economy were growing at a
normal rate, as defined above, and there were no special factors. Pct :

No assessment of the underlying balance of payments is strictly I
ingful without some assumption about the pressure of demand at a
the economy is being run. Quite apart from any more controyel
considerations, the lower the pressure of demand in any partict lar
the lower is the level of output and real incomes, and, therefore, the}
is the level of imports. Calculations of the underlying balance of paym
are in practice based (a) on the average pressure of demand ove!
period of years studied or (+) on the maximum level of unemployn
regarded as either politically tolerable or economically desirable, accor
to the fashion of the moment. Course (a) is at least unambiguous,
involves fewer hypothetical magnitudes. CNN Cd

The increase in gross current overseas official spending was almost exactly
sn line with the growth of the money national income. In fact military
commitments became so much more expensive in foreign exchange terms
that, although gross overseas military expenditure rose by nearly two-

commitments faster made the payments problem worse.
Private ‘invisibles’ were the one part of the balance of payments to show
| marked improvement. This was the net outcome of a number of forces |
working in different directions. A big drop in the surplus on certain |
traditional items, such as shipping, and a deterioration in the travel
balance were more than offset by a gain in net receipts on profits, interest y
and dividends. Overseas investment earnings were in fact far and away /
the most buoyant item in the British balance of payments. Unfortunately el
F the resulting improvement in the invisible balance was not enough to
outweigh the deterioration in the balance on goods and services. yl

BRITAIN’S COMPETITIVE POSITION /
Sooner or later any examination of the 19 gos and 1960s comes back
therefore to trade performance. This had two aspects: the large tise in
imports of manufactured goods and rapid fall in the British share of world
exports of manufactured goods shown in Fig. 6. Both can be regarded as ahs |
a decline in the ‘competitiveness’ of British goods. But it was the beha- '
| viour of exports rather than imports that was abnormal by international }
standards. The ratio of imports of manufactured goods to the Gross
| Domestic Product rose from 4-1 per cent in 1950 to.79 Pet cent in 1966. .
The export ratio on the other hand remained obstinately stuck at around
i 14 per cent. Other countries experienced similar or greater increase in the
1 appetite for imports, which were offset by a rising export ratioyf

It is most unlikely that the large discrepancies in price behaviour can be
explained away by the well-known deficiencies of export price indices. A ‘
breakdown of the UK and German price indices by the Board of Trade
shows that British prices increased more rapidly than Germany’s on a
sector-by-sector basis.* Differences in the commodity composition or :
weighting of the two indices thus do not seem to explain away the dis-
: crepancies. Some of the data are reproduced in Table 20. : |
British money wages rose in fact a great deal less than those of most \
' | Continental countries for the greater part of the period. Even jn Germany — |
in the 1950s, when the labour market was slack because of the inflow of }
refugees from the East, wages rose as fast as in Britain and in 1960~7 they \
rose a good deal faster. Such cost gains as the EEC countries enjoyed 1
were entirely due to more rapidly rising productivity. i )
The available cost data do not in fact provide a complete explanation of
the behaviour of relative export prices. One paradox, still to be resolved Ree |
is that in the second half of the period, from 1960 to 1967, British wage !
costs per unit of output were rising more slowly than those 7 Wa ae
competitors, including Germany, yet export prices rong le rs ps j
crepancy cannot be explained by raw material prices. Mori ae ; i \

The reason why EEC countries were able to hold down | their
prices so much better than Britain in the 1960s is a matter of gues
A plausible explanation is that EEC manufacturers started
previous decade with much more fat in their export profit margin
did British industrialists, and were thus better able ‘to abs orb |
labour costs. Another contributory factor may have been that t he de
international cost advantage enjoyed by Continental countries in the
led to a great expansion of export sales effort, involving the se tting
good dealer networks and service facilities, and the gearing © f bu
activity to the requirements of foreign markets. These aa 5
attitudes of mind did not disappear when costs rose and profit n na
narrowed. The result was that Continental manufacturers occup
entrenched position in world markets, which Britain found very di fi
to erode. ie ie 1A

management than the fact that it occurred and was fully docun nented
by year by the comparative movement of export shares and export p

| STERLING’S INTERNATIONAL ROLE |
No mention has yet been made of the international role of sterling, to :
; which Britain’s difficulties were often attributed. This international role
| had three different aspects: (a) the use of sterling as a reserve currency by
members of the sterling area; (b) the policy of encouraging banks, financial
institutions and commercial firms to hold large trading and working :
balances in London; and (¢) the fostering of the City as an international =
financial centre with gold, foreign exchange and commodity markets, '
where world trade that did not touch Britain’s shores could be financed |
and loans arranged for overseas borrowers. ico ‘
Although the three aspects are connected historically, they are 1 mal
degree separable. The pound’s position as a reserve currency is best seen
| in the movement of the sterling area’s own balances in London. ‘There was \ 3
no doubt that in the words of the BIS 1967-8 Report, ‘the persistent f
| disproportion between reserves and sterling Hiabilities could be an inde- ;
pendent source of pressure on the exchange rate.’ The knowledge we t
liabilities were hanging over Britain like a Sword of Damocles wae a
inhibited the country’s freedom of action. The fact remams that the th ia
was hardly ever realized before 1967. Figure 7 shows how stable ps big

The adverse reaction after 1967 reflected the desire to insure against a

difficulties. |
The balances held by non-sterling countries reflected the use of the |
pound as a trading (or private deposit) rather than as a reserve currency. j
__ Fig. 7 shows that these ‘other’ balances were mote volatile than those of |
the sterling area. (From 1961 onwards the chart figures conceal the true \
extent of this volatility as they include short-term central bank assistance |
to Britain.) London’s highly developed ability to attract such funds indeed +
had a damaging effect on policy, but in a way opposite to that often
: alleged. These short-term funds provided a cushion of ‘hot? money |
during periods of payments deficit which enabled Governments to post-
pone remedial action until a very late stage, thereby ensuring that the
action was harsher than would otherwise have been necessary and was
taken when the economy was already about to turn down. The ‘hot’ » |
money could have been kept out of the country in the first place by the |
simple expedient of maintaining lower interest tates, But at no time a |e
British reserves strong enough either to repel short-term? private fun ae |
to face with equanimity a net withdrawal of the official balances. b
alternative to relying on borrowed funds was to have replaced = ay 1
Teserves earned from a balance-of-payments surplus—an issue be fae 1
never faced by those who railed against ‘gnomes’, ‘speculators. ran

A resp" from a political point of ce ee
instore period there was strong relationship fees ae ee
Dowrolitical popularity. A multiple regression analysis carried at BaD
Fete Goodhart suggests that the political popularity of the Goin ;
ofthe day was highly sensitive, especially after a few months’ time-lag, to
quite small variations 1 the degree of unemployment. A shift of as little
as 50,000 OF 0°2 PEE cent could, other things being equal, have made a
| difference of up to 5 per cent to the Government’s lead over the Opposi-
Hon.* (It will be interesting to see what happens if unemployment per-
centages of 2 per cent oF above, which up to 1967 were anindex of recession, ;
prevail when consumption is rising reasonably rapidly.)
Equally, however, exchange rate variations were not free of both
economic and political costs. The idea was unpopular and the effects were
regarded as highly uncertain and possibly dangerous. Therefore it was
rational for Governments (a) to keep unemployment as low as possible
for as long as possible but (b) to deflate, whenever necessary to preserve
the sterling exchange rate, provided that there seemed a good chance of being
able to bring unemployment down again early enough before the next election.
Quite apart from any other difficulties this motivation prevented any
sustained experiment either in running the economy at a lower pressure
of demand, or at a more constant pressure free of the ratchet effects of
election booms, But the very pattern of the electoral policy cycle, with its
inflationary legacies, was itself highly likely to lead to an increase over the
years in the size of the underlying deficit; and thus ultimately bring about
a situation in which the risk that the Government might not be able to
bring down unemployment sufficiently by election day at the existing
exchange rate clearly and obviously outweighed the political risks and
disadvantages of devaluation. This point came in November 1967 (al-
though some members of the Cabinet believed that it had already arrived )
soon after the March 1966 election).

men who imagine they can control their destiny. Yet there are questions |
in the realm of the might-have-been which are interesting for their oY
sake and because the issues they raise are of relevance to the es - \
first is whether the balance-of-payments weakness actually held we e |
underlying growth rate, and the second is whether the whole sad story

But while the average pressure of demand over the period up to 1
Was not too low, the policy cycle described above almost certainly
have an adverse effect, aggravating all the other factors making for si lowe

hot to deny that they could have profitably moderated at an earlie r st tag

by a chronic payments deficit, which itself always cast a doubt on whe

Payments crises exerted a pessimistic influence on expectations eve i ae

The more interesting version of the ‘margin of slack’ concept, of which ] :
Professor Paish is the best-known exponent, is that Britain’s troubles | <=
would not have arisen if the economy had been run at a lower average | :
pressure of demand over the cycle as a whole. The recommended targets |
| have generally involved unemployment percentages in the 2-2'5 per cent \
' range. This would, of course, have meant higher rates both at cyclical |
peaks and in the heavy unemployment areas; and it must in fairness be ;
said that writers such as Professor Paish were strong advocates of measures | :
to even out regional unemployment variations, without being starry-eyed |
, about the practical possibilities. !
i If the average pressure of demand had been kept at a lower level from |
: the early 1950s onwards, Britain’s competitive position would probably |
have been a good deal easier. But protagonists of these ‘margin of slack’ ]
theories did not always follow the implications of their own ideas. For |
7 the failure to take their advice must surely have had, in their view, some |
effect on British money costs and prices. By the 1960s the balance of pay-
ments was ina state of fundamental disequilibrium, which could only have
been remedied by actually reducing British money costs and. prices
relative to those of other countries. To have accomplished this without
q devaluation would indeed have required, as suggested in Chapter 11, @
i much higher unemployment ratio than 2-2°5 per cent, The larger |
} ! ‘margin of slack’ theory is best regarded either as one that should have
been adopted many years before the 1967 devaluation, or as a recipe for
4 preventing a fresh devaluation in the future. It is not nearly as neat and |
= simple to operate as those who have merely picked up the slogan imagine. \

THE NEDC AND NATIONAL PLANS

The conventional case for ‘indicative planning’ often put emphasis on is
removing inconsistencies in the forward programmes of different sectors

of the economy. Brick production, for example, has not always been in

step with house building. Chemical investment has been held up through

lack of capacity on the part of chemical plant contractors.

1 This is not a strong argument. No one would deny that from time to .
time output has been held up, or imports sucked in, owing to particular :
bottlenecks; and it may be a good idea to attempt to improve the working

i of the market by organizing a greater interchange of information among

’ interested parties. But it is difficult to believe that inconsistencies due to

1 the lack of such information were responsible for more than a small part

| of Britain’s economic difficulties compared to the deterioration in the
country’s international competitive position, the heavy load of interna-
tional prestige commitments, or more deep-seated attitudes to change and
risk-taking. The National Plan, despite a great hunt for the ‘specific
areas of [physical] strain’, could only come out with the chemical plant
: example. :
Otherwise it provided some familiar information on shortages of skilled
manpower, which led into a discussion of Industrial Training Boards
(which were set up under an Act introduced by Mr Joseph Godber, the
previous Conservative Minister of Labour). But there was no suggestion
that anyone’s plans were being reconciled with anyone else's to ae
or take account of these shortages. One cannot imapme that seca
sentences such as ‘A rapid expansion is also required in the engin eae
-. industries’ were engaged in removing inconsistencies oF ms
— operational activity. !

i the demand for steel.* Forecasts are, of course, necessary in many private

of demand. Granted that any particular view is likely to be wrong, it is
just as arguable that—after exchanging opinions—each undertaking
should go ahead on its own view as that everyone should be wrong
together. If the Government's view of the future is regarded as having
particular authority, it will be in the self-interest of private concerns to
ay attention to it. ee .

‘The main justification for including in a ‘Plan’ large nu fee) i
;ndustry-by-industry projections interspersed with copious prose ee 7
short paper expressed in terms of the total GDP, with a few other broad |
aggregates, would strike most industrialists (and many business econo-
mists) as too abstract and insufficiently detailed. If they are to respond, the
national target must be translated into terms more relevant to their fa
industries. There is also the more cynical consideration that a big docu-
| ment full of figures is more likely (even if unread) to carry conviction than
j 4 short statement of objectives. But, contrary to what politicians and
‘practical men’ suppose, the detailed figures are in fact worked out from the
top downwards rather than from the bottom upwards; and this is neces-
sarily the case. ;
It should be obvious from what has already been said that a necessary
condition for the success of a national target, which depends on influencing
| business expectations, is an adequate growth of total demand. It is a
| measure of the unreality of most discussions of indicative planning that
| whole books are written on the subject in which the role of the Treasury
' and Finance Ministries is never mentioned. In Britain the Labour :
| Government of 1964 committed the economic howler of leaving the
Chancellor of the Exchequer off NEDC during the whole of the National :
Plan period on the pre-Keynesian grounds that that body was concerned
with physical planning, while the Chancellor was concerned with finance.
Of course the absence of the Chancellor in no way saved the Plan from 5
5 destruction. It simply made it easier for a time to gloss over the dis-
crepancy between what the Plan said and how the economy was actually
1 being run, and thus made the eventual disillusionment that much greater.
An experiment in raising expectations is doomed from the start if
businessmen do not believe that the Government will allow demand to
$ rise by anything like the projected rate, for balance-of-payments reasons.
vo This scepticism was there from the start, even before the alate
ne blow of July 1966 caused the National Plan to be formally abandoned. : y
e rational policy for accelerating the rate of growth would have had to |
= contain a strategy for maintaining 2 payments surplus when Outpit
te growing at that rate; and it was oben the start that this essen!
» a ingredient was missing from the Natio :
ne The measures of July 1966 were the final blow which destroyed all

THE ARGUMENT ABOUT EXCHANGE RATES
BENEVOLENT cynic could easily argue that Britain sho uld be pr,
Av President Nasser and to General Dayan for the - liddle
War, which magnified the 1967 deficit to a frighteningly high |
triggered off the succession of sterling crises which led to de "2
Without it the Government might have struggled on hopeles;
desperately for some time longer at the old $2-80 parity, kept g
injections of overseas credit and drifting gradually into a siege é
with no visible prospect of emerging into solvency, oe
This gratitude would have been greater if the war and th e at
sterling crises had come a little earlier. For the lateness of t
devaluation was not merely a matter of lost opportunities; as will bi
below it made the whole exercise more difficult, more risky an
unpleasant than it need otherwise have been, But more serious eve
the technical disadvantages of acting so late was the effect that it ]
public attitudes. ie
Some of the most popular arguments against any parity chang
example that it was impossible because other countries would fol lov
same degree—were later exploded by events. So was the belief th
parity change was ‘politically impossible’. But controversies of this
are never quite settled by evidence, and a whole fresh crop of misco
tions has been fostered by the circumstances surrounding the 1967 m
Devaluation was undoubtedly a defeat for the Labour Government a
for the more conservative sections of financial opinion; but it was wide
hailed as a defeat for the nation as well. A possible result of this feeli
together with the unpopularity of the Government in ‘the period th:
followed, could have been to reinforce the prejudice against exchange ra
changes until they are forced by events, Such a reinforcement woul
harmful. Even if foreign exchange is rolling into the reserves by the t
this book is published, it would take a very bold man to claim. that sterit
could never become overvalued again, as a result either of events in
country or parity changes abroad, Ne Bech !
Looking at the matter in a less insular way, the advisability of exchat
rate changes as a general method of adjusting the balan ay 4h C

| Most of the anti-devaluation arguments based on the check to real

deficit at all. A country wishing to cure a current payments deficit has by | SS

whether consumption actually has to fall, or there is just a lower rate of \ ne

(c) the deterioration in the terms of trade required to persuade other 1
countries to take more of the exports of the devaluing country and to
persuade its citizens to buy less from abroad. : ?
The success of the 1967 devaluation will take some time to assess. Itwill 2
depend on whether the right rate was chosen, on the general development
of world trade and payments, and on what other countries do Lats
; currencies; it would be absurd to hazard a guess which will be out of ca "
before the reader sees it. Nevertheless, the arithmetic of the 199) ae
worth examining. If the size of the change turns out to have Ree pe
quate, the argument against any attempt to have stuck to the $200 pa
will be reinforced. Ain on

The balance of payments uncertainties were even greater than the | :
domestic ones. Because of the unsatisfactory nature of the dividing line )
between short- and long-term capital and the greater volatility of the capital .
account, the emphasis of policy rightly switched to the current account.

The current deficit for 1968 appeared on the first estimate to be just =
over £400m., about the same as 1967. Despite a big improvement between ) :
the two halves of the year, it was still running at an annual rate of about .
£300m.—or about £200m. excluding US aircraft—at the end of 1968. The
overall deficit for the year was some £460m., only slightly better than in

1967. Yet the capital account was heavily influenced by aircraft loans

from the US Export/Import Bank, by another postponement of interest =
and capital payments on the postwar North American loans, and by ex- =
ceptional German deposits in an advance of ‘offset’ payments.

Is there anything one can say on the Treasurys : | .

has argued ‘that what exist in the real world are not such strange wei

“Sussex”, “industry”, “racing” or “cricket”, but just a collection of =
individuals who change from time to time, and their views, habits and i oS
ideas with them.’* Although not a complete account of the matter, the =
point needs to be made. =
To the extent that ‘the Treasury’ has an objective existence, the only :
appropriate attitude to it is that of ambivalence. It suffers from the ! SSS
defects of most highly inbred organizations of intelligent men, which s es =
have been noted in Chapter 1, where some suggested changes are dis- ——
cussed. But whether they are carried out or not, it is always safe to ERs
assume that any governmental institution requires stimulus, challenge =~
and even provocation from outside. ; —

to its readiness to use Keynesian techniques to regulate the overall i ;
of spending. In these respects it had a lead of many years over o of its

= irrelevant and only delivery dates, salesmanship or design Matter, has beg
y answered in the preceding chapters. Two other points are worth adding
= The first is that opposition to changes in the exchange rate is self contra
dictory, when accompanied, as is frequently the case, by a strong bel; icf
an incomes policy and/or a higher margin of unemployment to hold dow
money costs and prices. For all such policies, were they to work, would

would make no sense if neither price nor profitability changes could b; ring
about adjustment, ' hid
The second, and more important, argument against exchange Tate
changes is based on a questionable philosophy of government. The level
of home demand and exchange rates are the two areas where the Govern.
ment has the power to act, and to influence events fairly quickly (although
not overnight). Yet the opponents of exchange rate changes reject action
in the second of these two areas and think they can act instead (too often
through exhortation) on the Presumed deep-seated factors such as
managerial efficiency and industrial attitudes, where the Government | las.
no direct power or Tesponsibility, and any encouragement it can giye will
take many years to produce an effect, Paradoxically, it was the refusal of
governments in the 1950s and 1960s to act in areas where they can have a
quick and indisputable Influence that created the panics and abrupt
changes of policy which impeded the effectiveness of the long-term
‘fundamental’ policies for which so much was claimed, a
The view that a flexible rate was impossible for a Teserve-currency —

had not developed, Considerable overseas balances were held in London

might have been accepted by the sterling-area countries if it had come —
earlier and had been accompanied by more convincing internal measures
from the beginning. The Tational time to move funds out of London is — i
before rather than after a devaluation, unless a second devaluation is,
considered a serious tisk, : ie ae
